Chapter 12 · Inequalities and active constraints

Translate one-sided equilibrium into complementarity equations, identify an active set and verify both force and feasibility.

From first principles

Express one-sided contact as a complementarity condition

Consider a point constrained to have gap \(g\ge0\) above a frictionless rigid stop. A compressive reaction \(p\ge0\) can prevent penetration, but a separated stop exerts no force. These requirements give the scalar conditions

\[g\ge0,\qquad p\ge0,\qquad gp=0 .\]

They allow either an open gap with zero force or closed contact with a nonnegative force. To connect this with optimization, suppose the physical coordinate is the gap itself and the unconstrained stored energy is differentiable and convex. At a constrained minimum on \(g\ge0\), every feasible first-order change has nonnegative energy change. Thus the equilibrium derivative obeys

\[E'(g)=p,\qquad p\ge0,\]
\[gE'(g)=0 .\]

In the interior one may move in both directions, forcing zero derivative. At the boundary only increases in gap are feasible, allowing positive derivative. The balance equation for a specific spring or load supplies the remaining relationship needed to determine the gap and force.

Background · Complementarity
\[x\ge0,\quad y\ge0,\quad xy=0\]

Both quantities are nonnegative and at least one vanishes. Active-set changes can make a smooth Newton model inappropriate across the switch.

Derivation and applications

Quantify the gap introduced by smoothing a contact condition

Algebraic regularization can replace the sharp switch between an open and a closed contact with a differentiable approximation. First scale the gap and reaction into dimensionless variables \(g,p\), using stated positive reference scales. One exact residual is \(\sqrt{g^2+p^2}-g-p\): setting it to zero implies \(g+p\ge0\), and squaring gives \(gp=0\), which together force nonnegativity. Introduce a smoothing parameter \(\mu>0\),

\[\phi_\mu(g,p)=\sqrt{g^2+p^2+2\mu}-g-p .\]

At a zero, \(g+p>0\), and squaring gives \(gp=\mu\). Thus both quantities are positive even where the physical contact would be exactly open or closed. For a one-degree-of-freedom linear balance \(p=k(g-g_0)\) with positive scaled stiffness \(k\) and unloaded gap \(g_0\), solve the positive quadratic root:

\[k g(g-g_0)=\mu,\]
\[g_\mu=\frac{g_0+\sqrt{g_0^2+4\mu/k}}{2},\]
\[p_\mu=\frac{\mu}{g_\mu}.\]

For an exactly grazing load \(g_0=0\), the smoothing affects gap and force on square-root scales,

\[g_\mu=\sqrt{\mu/k},\]
\[p_\mu=\sqrt{k\mu}.\]

Far from this switching point the smaller of the two quantities decays linearly with \(\mu\). A small residual of the smoothed equation only proves that the regularized balance has been solved. The value of \(\mu\) and the physical reference scales determine the discrepancy from exact contact.

Worked example

Measure parasitic force across an open gap

Let \(k=4,\ g_0=0.50,\ \mu=0.010\). The exact unsmoothed balance is

\[g=0.50,\ p=0.\]

The smoothed solution gives

\[g_\mu=(0.50+\sqrt{0.26})/2=0.50495,\]

with \(p_\mu=0.01980\). Multiplication gives \(g_\mu p_\mu=0.0100\), an independent check on the source of this residual force.

Second application

Choose smoothing at a grazing contact

With \(k=9\) and dimensionless admissible gap error \(0.002\), a grazing contact requires \(\sqrt{\mu/9}\le0.002\). Thus \(\mu\le3.60\times10^{-5}\). At this upper value the induced reaction is \(0.0180\). If the admissible reaction is only \(0.009\), the stricter requirement is

\[\mu\le0.009^2/9=9.0\times10^{-6}.\]

A stop can push in only one direction

A constrained coordinate \(u\) must remain nonnegative and has an elastic energy \(E(u)=ku^2/2-fu\), \(k>0\). Minimising a differentiable energy on a half-line gives an interior stationary point when \(u>0\). At the endpoint zero, only nonnegative variations are possible, so the one-sided derivative must be nonnegative. Introduce the reaction \(w=ku-f\). These conditions read \(u\ge0,\ w\ge0,\ uw=0\). They express that at least one of gap and reaction vanishes. A similar rule applies componentwise to a vector of gaps coupled through a stiffness matrix.

The scalar solution is

\[u=\max(f/k,0),\]
\[w=\max(-f,0).\]

Increasing \(f\) through zero changes the slope of the load–displacement graph. The solution stays continuous while its derivative jumps. Near that change an unconstrained differentiable root solver can produce a negative gap or an inadmissible attractive reaction unless its equations include the inequalities.

Couple two potential contact points

Let \(K=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}2&-1\\-1&2\end{smallmatrix}\right)\), \(f=(-1,1)^T\), \(w=Ku-f\) and \(u_i\ge0\). The unconstrained equilibrium gives \(u=K^{-1}f=(-1/3,1/3)^T\), violating the first gap. Test the set with \(u_1=0\), \(w_2=0\). The second equation becomes \(2u_2-1=0\), hence \(u_2=1/2\). Recover the first reaction \(w_1=-1/2+1=1/2\). Every gap and reaction is now nonnegative and every product vanishes. Positive definiteness of \(K\) makes the quadratic energy strictly convex, so a feasible stationary state satisfying these inequalities is its unique minimum.

For a changed load \(f=(-0.4,1)^T\), the same assumed active set yields \(w_1=-0.1\), which is inadmissible. Release the first coordinate and solve \(Ku=f\). Using \(K^{-1}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}2&1\\1&2\end{smallmatrix}\right)/3\) gives \(u=(1/15,8/15)^T\), positive in both coordinates, with zero reactions. A small change of loading has switched the equations that determine the state.

A residual for switching equations

After compatible nondimensionalisation, \(\min(u_i,w_i)=0\) is equivalent to the three componentwise complementarity conditions. Where \(u_i<w_i\), linearising the minimum selects the gap equation; where \(w_i<u_i\), it selects the equilibrium equation. At equality the derivative is not unique, which explains the need for a switching rule or a nonsmooth Newton formulation. A product alone is insufficient: a negative gap and zero reaction give a zero product. Report the largest feasibility violation and a complementarity residual together. At the scalar switching load \(f=0\), both gap and reaction vanish and the active designation is ambiguous even though the state itself is unique.

Read the reaction as the price of a constraint

Return to a single gap with stiffness 2 and load −4. Its minimum over \(u\ge0\) occurs at zero with reaction 4. Now move the lower stop to \(u\ge d\) for a small positive \(d\). The constrained minimum becomes \(E(d)=d^2+4d\), whose derivative at zero is 4, precisely the original reaction. A small imposed displacement of the active stop requires work to first order. For a positive load producing a strictly open gap, the same small stop shift would initially have no effect and the reaction would be zero. These energy changes provide an independent check on the reaction sign.

Derive a discrete obstacle problem from virtual work

Consider a membrane represented by three interior displacements with fixed zero displacements at its ends. In scaled units the elastic energy is one half the sum of squared neighbouring differences, including the terms at both supports. Its stiffness matrix has diagonal two and neighbouring entries minus one. An obstacle at height \(g_j\) requires

\[u_j\ge g_j.\]

Minimising \(E(u)=u^TKu/2-f^Tu\) over this convex feasible set gives a simple test involving every feasible variation: \((Ku-f)^T(v-u)\ge0\) for every feasible \(v\).

To see necessity, move a small fraction of the distance from \(u\) toward \(v\) and differentiate the energy at zero step. For sufficiency, the exact energy difference equals that linear term plus \((v-u)^TK(v-u)/2\), which is nonnegative and strictly positive for a nonzero displacement because both endpoints are fixed. At an interior free node both signs of variation are allowed, so its reaction vanishes. At an active obstacle only upward variations are feasible, so the signed reaction is nonnegative. These properties give complementarity between the gap \(u-g\) and the reaction \(Ku-f\).

Determine which part of an obstacle actually supplies force

Let \(g=(0,1,0)^T\), \(f=0\). Setting all displacements equal to the obstacle would give

\[Ku=(-1,2,-1)^T,\]

with negative endpoint reactions at the first and third interior nodes. Those two active guesses are inadmissible. Keep only the middle node in contact and solve free-node equilibrium: \(2u_1-u_2=0\) and \(2u_3-u_2=0\). With \(u_2=1\) this yields \(u=(0.5,1,0.5)^T\), whose reactions are \((0,1,0)^T\). The end gaps are both positive and the central gap is zero, satisfying the complete optimality test.

The energy is half the sum of four squared slopes \(0.5,0.5,-0.5,-0.5\), hence 0.5. The candidate that hugs the entire obstacle has slopes \(0,1,-1,0\) and energy one. Lifting the two free nodes lets the membrane distribute curvature more economically while remaining above the obstacle. Independently the sum of the support reactions balances the unit central contact force, since each outer spring slope has magnitude 0.5 in these scaled units.

Apply an additional positive force \(f_2=t\) at the centre while keeping the obstacle fixed. The contact-state reaction becomes \(1-t\). Up to \(t=1\) the position stays fixed and the contact load decreases. For \(t>1\) the contact can no longer sustain a negative reaction; solving the unconstrained equations gives \(u=(t/2,t,t/2)^T\), now above every obstacle node. This piecewise response exhibits an observable change of slope in displacement at the contact transition. Reusing the old active set without checking the sign of its reaction would incorrectly predict an unchanging displacement for arbitrarily large lifting forces.

A force test and a gap test remain necessary even when the computed energy looks sensible. For example \(u=(0.4,1,0.4)^T\) is feasible and has energy only 0.52, close to the optimal 0.5. Yet its outer reactions are each −0.2 at strictly open gaps. The complementarity equations identify the reducible energy error even without knowing the exact energy minimum.

Locate the switch between free motion and contact

A unit-stiffness spring has free displacement equal to applied force but a rigid stop enforces \(x\le1\). With contact force \(r\ge0\) the force balance is \(x+r=f\) and complementarity gives \(r(1-x)=0\). Examine each active set.

Problem sheet 6 · Misfit and one-sided equilibrium

Distinguish a stationary residual fit from an exact root and identify which unilateral constraints carry load.

Fit incompatible sensors

Two dimensionless residuals are

\[r_1=x-2,\ r_2=2x-3.\]

Compute the exact least-squares state, both residuals and their dot product with the Jacobian. How many undamped Gauss–Newton steps suffice from zero?

Worked solution

The gradient is \((x-2)+2(2x-3)=5x-8\), giving \(x=1.6\). The residual is \((-0.4,0.2)^T\) and the dot product with \((1,2)^T\) is zero.

The residual is affine, so its linear model is exact everywhere. One step from zero produces 1.6 even though neither individual observation is exactly satisfied.

Try this calculation. Change one observation’s uncertainty and recompute the weighted projection. More than one fitted coefficient requires the additional basis columns specified in this exercise.

Open this adjustable calculation

A stationary point with a poor model

For the scalar residual \(r=x^2-1\), evaluate the residual, gradient and curvature of \(\Phi=r^2/2\) at zero. Analyse the undamped Gauss–Newton linear least-squares subproblem there, and compare it with a trial step to \(x=0.1\).

Worked solution

At zero \(r=-1\), \(J=0\) and \(Jr=0\). Exact curvature \(J^2+rr''=-2\) identifies a strict local maximum.

The model \(r+Js=-1\) has the same size for every step, so cannot select a descent direction. Directly testing \(x=0.1\) gives \(\Phi=0.99^2/2=0.49005\), below 0.5. A small gradient alone cannot certify a minimum.

Explore the related calculation with adjustable inputs. Change the parameters in the corresponding calculation and compare its independently recomputed values with the equation derived in this solution.

Locate the switching load

Use \(K=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}3&-1\\-1&2\end{smallmatrix}\right)\), \(f=(t,2)^T\), \(u,w\ge0\), \(w=Ku-f\), \(u_iw_i=0\). Assume the first gap is closed. Determine the range of \(t\) for which that assumption holds and solve the case \(t=0\).

Worked solution

With \(u_1=0\), the open second gap has \(2u_2=2\), so \(u_2=1\). The first reaction is \(-1-t\), nonnegative precisely for \(t\le-1\). At the threshold the closed coordinate has zero reaction.

At \(t=0\) release the first gap. Solving \(3u_1-u_2=0\), \(-u_1+2u_2=2\) gives \((0.4,1.2)^T\) with both reactions zero. The positive gaps verify the new active set.

Explore the related calculation with adjustable inputs. Inspect the active-set switch in the scalar contact example. The two-coordinate exercise still requires checking both inequalities with its stated stiffness matrix.

Locate and follow the two equilibria of a fold

Consider equilibria of the dimensionless evolution u′ = λ − u². For λ = 0.04 find both roots, classify their stability, and estimate the movement of the positive root when λ increases by 0.002. Then find out why stepping in λ fails near zero and construct a continuation step through the fold.

Worked solution

The equilibria are u = ±0.2. A small perturbation obeys δu′ ≈ −2u δu, so +0.2 attracts while −0.2 repels. Both are perfectly valid zeros of the steady residual. A solver’s ability to converge to either root has no direct implication for dynamical stability.

Differentiating u²−λ = 0 gives du/dλ = 1/(2u). At the positive root the predicted change is +0.005. The exact change is √0.042 − 0.2 ≈ 0.00494. At the negative root the sign reverses. As the fold approaches, sensitivity diverges even though the equilibrium curve itself is the smooth parabola λ = u².

At (u,λ) = (0,0) choose the tangent along increasing u. An arclength predictor of length 0.05 gives (0.05,0). Keep the correction normal to that tangent by imposing u = 0.05 together with the residual. The corrected point is (0.05,0.0025). The two-equation Jacobian remains invertible there and at the fold. A parameter sweep fixing λ below zero would have no real point to correct to.

Explore the related calculation with adjustable inputs. Use the equilibrium parabola to inspect sensitivity and the tangent-coordinate continuation step. Translate the state coordinate if the fold is centred at a different value here.

Chapter 13 · Why a flat zero slows Newton's method

Factor a residual near a multiple root, recover the missing factor in a Newton step and distinguish a small residual from a small location error.

Expand around the actual root

Write \(f(x)=(x-r)^m g(x)\), with \(g\) smooth and \(g(r)\ne0\). Denote the current error by \(e=x-r\). Differentiation gives

\[f'=e^{m-1}(m g+eg').\]

Away from the root the quotient stays finite even when both function and derivative become very small. Substitution into Newton's update gives

\[e_{+}=e-\frac{e g}{m g+eg'}=(1-1/m)e+O(e^2).\]

If \(m=1\), the first-order error cancels. If \(m>1\), a fixed fraction survives. Multiplying the correction by the known multiplicity gives \(e_+=e^2g'/(mg+eg')\), restoring local quadratic convergence. A wrong value of \(m\) can send steps across the root or make them diverge.

A contact measurement

Near tangency a gap can behave like \(f=(x-2)^2\) in dimensionless coordinates. Starting from 3 produces 2.5, 2.25 and 2.125. The gap residual falls by a factor of four per step. The location error only halves. A stopping rule \(|f|<10^{-8}\) certifies merely \(|x-2|<10^{-4}\) in this exact model. The multiplicity-corrected step reaches 2 immediately.

Now take \(f=e^2(1+e)\) at \(e=0.1\). The residual and derivative are 0.011 and 0.23. An ordinary correction leaves error 0.05217. Twice the correction leaves 0.00435. The analytic denominator explains why this second update is no longer exact when \(g\) varies.

Compare errors over twelve iterations

Use \(f=e^m(1+e)\) with positive starting error. Follow the ordinary and multiplicity-corrected ratios without forming underflowing powers. The logarithmic display stops at a floor of \(10^{-15}\). Check the slope of the ordinary iteration after the first few steps.

Open this experiment online

Tutorial on error certificates

A zero of \(f=e^4\) has residual \(10^{-12}\). How many decimal places of location accuracy follow from this observation alone?

Worked solution

The magnitude of the location error is \(10^{-3}\). Four powers have compressed the residual. Any inference based on a nonzero lower bound for \(|f'|\) fails across this root.

Why might a sign-changing bracket never isolate a double root?

Worked solution

The leading sign is the sign of \(g(r)\) on both sides. A sampler can step across the root without a sign change. Information about stationary points or a separate lower-bound argument is needed.

Read the university treatment

Compare the error analysis at a simple root with the extra multiplicity factor derived here. Driscoll and Braun · Solving nonlinear equations

Study the full derivation

Count enclosed zeros with a contour before locating them · Nonlinear systems and iterative methods

A complex residual may have several nearby zeros whose Newton basins are hard to distinguish. Use the argument principle to count them, contour moments to estimate their locations and an independent perturbation test to certify a root cluster.

Chapter 14 · Eliminating a fixed point's leading geometric error

Recover a limit from three consecutive iterates, derive Steffensen's update and find the cancellation that makes an apparently faster step unreliable.

Three readings of an unknown limit

Suppose \(x_n=r+cq^n\) with unknown \(r,c,q\). Consecutive differences are \(c(q-1)q^n\) and \(c(q-1)q^{n+1}\). Their ratio estimates \(q\). Substituting that ratio into the sum of the geometric tail gives Aitken's delta-squared estimate

\[\widehat r=x_n-\frac{(x_{n+1}-x_n)^2}{x_{n+2}-2x_{n+1}+x_n}.\]

For an exactly geometric sequence the denominator is \(cq^n(q-1)^2\) and the cancellation is exact. For a smooth fixed-point map \(g\) with \(|g'(r)|<1\), the leading error is locally geometric, with a quadratic remainder. Replacing \(x_n,x_{n+1},x_{n+2}\) by \(x,g(x),g(g(x))\), then restarting at the estimate, gives Steffensen's iteration. Each outer step costs two map evaluations.

Two computed limits

For \(g(x)=1+0.8(x-1)\) and \(x=0\), the three numbers are 0, 0.2 and 0.36. The denominator is \(-0.04\). The accelerated value is

\[0-0.04/(-0.04)=1.\]

A slowly contracting map can supply a very accurate extrapolation when the data are exact.

To solve \(x=\cos x\), begin at 0.5. The next two map evaluations are approximately 0.87758 and 0.63901. The accelerated value is about 0.73139. Its fixed-point residual is much smaller than the initial \(\cos(0.5)-0.5\), but plugging the result back into the original equation is essential: the geometric model is now only local.

Compare by number of map evaluations

Use the smooth map \(g(1+e)=1+qe+0.05e^2\) close to its fixed point. Plain substitution gets one update per function call. The accelerated curve receives two calls per update. Read the horizontal axis before deciding which method made better use of the budget.

Open this experiment online

Tutorial on stability of the transformation

For an exact linear map with \(q=0.999\) and error one, what order of magnitude is the second difference?

Worked solution

Its magnitude is \((1-q)^2=10^{-6}\), while the three original readings may be of order one. A measurement perturbation comparable to this denominator can completely change the extrapolation.

What should an implementation do at an exact fixed point where all three readings agree?

Worked solution

Return that fixed point after checking the residual. The delta-squared expression is then \(0/0\). Away from a certified fixed point, a small denominator warrants retaining a verified plain step or a different local model.

Study the full derivation

Accelerating fixed points with differences of residuals · Nonlinear systems and iterative methods

A slowly convergent physical iteration contains information in the direction of successive errors. Use a scalar extrapolation and a small residual-minimisation problem to exploit that information, then test the extrapolated state against the original equation.

Chapter 15 · Deciding how far to trust a linearised residual

Derive an acceptance test from predicted versus actual decrease and see how it rescues a Newton direction that crosses the root by a large distance.

Choosing a step from a local model

For a scalar residual \(f\), finding a zero also minimises \(\phi=f^2/2\) if a zero is attainable. Near \(x\), approximate \(f(x+s)\) by \(f(x)+f'(x)s\), yielding a quadratic model \(m(s)=(f+f's)^2/2\). Minimise this model subject to \(|s|\le\Delta\). In one dimension this clips the ordinary Newton step to the interval; when \(f'=0\), the model contains no preferred direction.

Measure both predicted and actual decrease. Provided the predicted decrease is positive, define

\[\rho=\frac{\phi(x)-\phi(x+s)}{m(0)-m(s)}.\]

A ratio near one supports the local model. A negative ratio means that a predicted gain produced an actual loss. Shrink the radius and repeat from the same point. A successful boundary step can justify enlarging the next radius. These decisions require an extra residual evaluation and do not require a globally accurate Taylor approximation.

Background · Trust radius
\[\|s\|\le\Delta\]

The local model is minimised on this region. Agreement between predicted and achieved reduction guides the next radius.

Two proposals for the same equation

For \(f(x)=x^2-2\) at \(x=0.2\), \(f=-1.96\), \(f'=0.4\). The full Newton proposal \(s=4.9\) lands at \(5.1\), where the residual is 24.01. The linear model predicted zero. The merit function rises catastrophically.

With radius one, \(s=1\) gives \(x=1.2\) and \(f=-0.56\). Predicted merit decrease is

\[(1.96^2-1.56^2)/2=0.704.\]

Actual decrease is

\[(1.96^2-0.56^2)/2=1.764.\]

The ratio exceeds one because the curvature accelerated the improvement. A large ratio is possible and has a direct interpretation.

Look along the step before accepting it

Compare the true merit function and the local model along positive steps from a starting value below the root. Move the trust radius and read the ratio for the constrained minimiser. Locate a region in which predicted and observed changes have opposite signs.

Tutorial on choosing a merit function

Start at zero for the same equation. Can reducing the radius find a direction from this particular model?

Worked solution

Here \(f'=0\) while \(f=-2\). The linearised residual is constant, hence every step predicts zero decrease. The ratio is undefined. A curvature model, perturbation, or another starting point is needed.

Is a small gradient of \(f^2/2\) sufficient to certify a root?

Worked solution

Its gradient is \(ff'\). It can vanish either at a root or at a stationary residual. Report the original residual and test feasibility to distinguish these outcomes.

Follow the nonlinear least-squares section to compare damping a proposed step with solving a constrained local subproblem. Driscoll and Braun · Nonlinear equations

Chapter 16 · A physically positive root across six decades

Reparametrise the unknown and rescale the residual in a nonlinear resistance model. Follow what each choice changes in a Newton step.

Start from a positive constitutive quantity

An ideal thermistor calibration can be written \(R/R_*=e^z\). More generally a sensor fit may require a positive amplitude \(x\) satisfying its logarithmic reading \(\log x=d\). A Newton correction on \(f(x)=\log x-d\) gives

\[x_+=x[1+d-\log x].\]

Even with a unique perfectly regular root \(e^d\), this formula can propose a negative amplitude when the initial residual exceeds one. Evaluating the next logarithm would then be impossible.

Choose \(z=\log x\) instead. The equation in the new variable is \(z=d\) and one step yields the answer. For an arbitrary residual \(f(x)\), chain differentiation gives

\[d f(e^z)/dz=x f'(x).\]

Newton moves \(z\) by \(-f/(xf')\), so the update in \(x\) is multiplicative:

\[x_+=x\exp[-f(x)/(x f'(x))].\]

Near a simple root, expanding the exponential gives the ordinary first-order correction; the difference is quadratic in the relative step. A logarithmic coordinate enforces positivity at every finite iterate. It cannot restore information missing from the measurement.

Measure failure and sensitivity separately

At \(d=0\), \(x_0=10\) gives \(x_1=10(1-\log 10)\approx-13.03\) with additive Newton. Log coordinates give \(x_1=1\). Start instead at 0.1. Additive Newton gives about 0.3303 and remains admissible. Positivity depends on the proposal relative to its scale.

If \(d=\log(1000)\) with measurement uncertainty 0.01, the parameter is about 1000 with relative uncertainty 1%. The direct sensitivity follows by differentiating the equation: \(dx/dd=x\). The same 0.01 measurement error near \(x=0.001\) means an absolute error near \(10^{-5}\), again about 1%. Changing variables improves interpretation of a relative uncertainty, without changing the information in the observation.

Inspect a Newton proposal without losing its invalid values

The ordinate shows the next candidate divided by the exact positive root. The horizontal axis is the starting ratio. The additive curve crosses zero at \(e\); the logarithmic update reaches the root from every displayed start. Move the marker to read the actual candidates for a chosen target scale.

Open this experiment online

Tutorial on relative residuals

A model supplies \(F(x)=e^{0.1x}\) and a positive observation \(y\). How do residuals \(F-y\) and \(\log F-\log y\) weight small changes near a fit?

Worked solution

Near \(F=y\),

\[\log F-\log y\approx(F-y)/y.\]

A squared log residual weights errors approximately by relative error. Choosing it implies a different observation-noise model when fitting several readings.

If the true parameter can be exactly zero, what does the logarithmic coordinate miss?

Worked solution

The zero boundary is at \(z=-\infty\). A finite transformed iterate cannot represent it. One must allow a boundary solution explicitly when the physics admits it.

Chapter 17 · Finding a turning point without using the load as a coordinate

Use tangent geometry to continue across a fold, and distinguish branch sensitivity from an ill-posed choice of variable in an equilibrium computation.

Two equilibria share one control value

Use the scaled normal form \(F(x,p)=x^2+p=0\). The equilibrium curve is \(p=-x^2\). At \(p=-0.01\) two displacements, \(x=\pm0.1\), give the same load. Direct continuation in \(p\) uses \(dx/dp=-F_p/F_x=-1/(2x)\). The derivative diverges at the fold. Plotting \(p\) against \(x\), however, produces a smooth curve there.

Background · Backward error
\[F(\hat x)+\delta F=0\]

Specify how perturbations in data or equations are measured. A tiny perturbation making the computed value exact can coexist with a large solution error near a fold.

Add a geometric condition

At a known point \(y_0=(x_0,p_0)\), let \(t\) be a unit tangent perpendicular to \((2x_0,1)\). A predictor \(y_*=y_0+\Delta s\,t\) lies on the tangent. Correct it by solving \(F(x,p)=0\) together with \(t\cdot(y-y_*)=0\), which confines corrections to a line normal to the old tangent. At the fold the Jacobian of this pair is

\[J=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\t_x&t_p\end{pmatrix},\qquad \det J=-t_x.\]

With the tangent aligned with the displacement axis, \(t_x\) is nonzero. The pair remains locally invertible where load control alone fails. This is a change in the computation's coordinate, not removal of the physical multiple equilibria at a fixed load.

A step that crosses the maximum

Start at \(x_0=-0.1,p_0=-0.01\) with increasing \(x\), so \(t=(1,0.2)/\sqrt{1.04}\). A step of length 0.2 predicts \(x_*\simeq0.0961,p_*\simeq0.0292\), which is off the curve and has positive load. The correction line satisfies

\[x+0.2p=x_*+0.2p_*.\]

Substitute \(p=-x^2\) to obtain \(x-0.2x^2\simeq0.1020\). The nearby root is about 0.1041 and the corrected load about −0.0108. Both endpoints have negative load despite crossing the maximum.

Test the predictor before trusting it

Choose the starting displacement and step length. Plot the tangent segment, the original curve and the selected nearby intersection of the correction line with the curve. A large step makes the tangent less faithful. The correction must stay on the intended local branch.

Open this experiment online

Tutorial on the experimental meaning

At \(p=-0.01\), a pressure measurement has uncertainty 0.001. Estimate its local displacement uncertainty and compare it near \(p=-0.0001\).

Worked solution

At \(|x|=0.1\), \(|dx/dp|=5\) and the linear estimate is 0.005. At \(|x|=0.01\), the formal factor is 50. The same pressure error now exceeds the distance to the fold, so the local linear approximation itself loses validity.

If the continuation algorithm traces both sides smoothly, are both equilibria dynamically stable?

Worked solution

A static residual gives equilibrium locations. Stability requires the linearisation of a specified evolution law or a second variation of the appropriate energy. Smooth numerical traversal gives no such certificate.

Chapter 18 · Newton's method for an unknown covariance square root

A pair of independent unit-variance signals must be transformed to a prescribed covariance ellipse. One can solve a scalar square-root equation along each principal axis. A calibration that starts in a different frame exposes the genuinely matrix-valued derivative. Derive it, solve for its correction, and determine which residual will detect a wrong update.

Translate a covariance requirement into an equation

Suppose \(z\) is a zero-mean random vector with identity covariance. Let a deterministic device produce \(y=Xz\). Multiplying out \(yy^T\) and averaging gives

\[\mathbb E(yy^T)=X\mathbb E(zz^T)X^T=XX^T.\]

A target covariance \(A\) is symmetric and positive semidefinite by construction. Restrict the exercise to positive definite targets. They correspond to genuine variation in every direction and allow us to discuss an invertible square root. The notebook uses two dimensionless readout channels, so mixing their coordinates has a defined physical meaning.

If the sole purpose were generating random samples, any factor satisfying \(XX^T=A\) would do. Indeed, replacing \(X\) by \(XQ\) for an orthogonal \(Q\) leaves this output covariance alone. We make an extra modelling decision: the calibration transform should be symmetric and positive. This picks a distinguished factor that stretches along principal axes and introduces no orthogonal rotation. The equation becomes \(X^2=A\). A Cholesky factor produces the correct covariance too, but squaring that generally triangular factor is a different calculation.

To see existence, diagonalise \(A=Q\Lambda Q^T\). The matrix \(S=Q\sqrt{\Lambda}Q^T\) squares to \(A\). For uniqueness on the positive branch, a putative root commutes with its own square and consequently preserves each eigenspace of \(A\). Within an eigenspace of eigenvalue \(a>0\), a symmetric positive root has only positive eigenvalues whose squares equal \(a\). It must act there as \(\sqrt a\) times the identity. Repeated eigenvalues leave the choice of basis in that subspace free while the positive root itself stays fixed.

Differentiate before choosing a linear solver

For scalar data, expanding \((x+h)^2=x^2+2xh+h^2\) supplies Newton's familiar correction. For matrices write every factor in its original order. The cross terms are \(XH\) and \(HX\). They coincide only under a commutation condition. The first variation is therefore a linear map on a whole matrix, not multiplication by a single derivative matrix on the left.

\[F(X)=X^2-A,\]
\[F(X+H)=F(X)+XH+HX+H^2.\]

Set the linearised residual to zero. The correction \(H\) solves the Sylvester equation \(XH+HX=A-X^2\). If \(X\) and the right-hand side are symmetric, the transpose of any solution satisfies the same equation. Existence and uniqueness on the positive branch therefore guarantee a symmetric correction. This argument is a practical implementation check: an update with substantial antisymmetric part could not have been obtained by solving this particular Newton equation accurately.

Diagonalise the current iterate \(X=U\,\mathrm{diag}(d_i)U^T\). Write a tilde for the representation in the \(U\) basis. The \(ij\) entry of \(XH+HX\) becomes \((d_i+d_j)\widetilde H_{ij}\). We can solve entry by entry and transform back. All denominators are positive if \(X\) is positive. The minimum denominator is \(2d_{\min}\); as the smallest stretch approaches zero, a fixed residual can request a large correction in a weakly varying direction. This sensitivity also appears when estimating a covariance from data near a rank loss.

\[\widetilde R=U^T(A-X^2)U,\]
\[\widetilde H_{ij}=\frac{\widetilde R_{ij}}{d_i+d_j},\]
\[X_{\mathrm{new}}=X+U\widetilde H U^T.\]

A tilted measurement that defeats the scalar update

Choose the axes of the current iterate for a hand calculation and take \(X=\operatorname{diag}(2,1)\). Suppose the target covariance measured in these axes is \(A=\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}5&1\\1&2\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)\). It is positive because its determinant is nine and its first diagonal entry is positive. Then \(R=A-X^2\) has all four entries equal to one. The diagonal corrections are \(1/4\) and \(1/2\). Both off-diagonal corrections equal \(1/3\), so the update rotates the stretching axes as well as changing their magnitudes.

What if one used \(2XH=R\)? The two off-diagonal entries of that candidate would be \(1/4\) and \(1/2\) instead. It fails even the requirement that the correction be symmetric. Its upper off-diagonal Sylvester residual is

\[3/4-1=-1/4.\]

A code that checks only whether \(2XH-R\) vanishes would report success on exactly the wrong derivative. A well-chosen verification problem needs noncommuting matrices. If both the initial and target matrices are diagonal in the same basis, the extra terms disappear from this test.

With the correct correction, substitution into the expanded formula gives \(F(X+H)=H^2\) in exact arithmetic. This provides a surprisingly inexpensive check: solve one linear system, form the new nonlinear residual, and compare it with the square of the correction. For the hand example the first diagonal entry of \(H^2\) equals

\[1/16+1/9=25/144.\]

The initial value of \(F_{11}\) was \(-1\). There is no reason every residual component should decrease at every remote initial condition, but this one-step identity holds regardless of the starting error.

Which errors does quadratic convergence describe?

Near the desired root, the inverse Sylvester map is bounded if the smallest eigenvalue of the iterate stays away from zero. In Frobenius norm the correction obeys \(\|H\|_F\le\|R\|_F/(2d_{\min})\). Together with submultiplicativity and \(F(X+H)=H^2\), this yields a bound on the next residual proportional to the square of the preceding one. The proportionality constant depends on the current scale. This explains the near-doubling of correct digits late in the calculation and also why such a rule cannot be assumed for an arbitrary ill-conditioned target from the first step.

For this particular problem even preservation of the positive branch can be understood. Rearrange the equation for \(Y=X+H\) to give

\[XY+YX=A+X^2.\]

The right side is positive. In an eigenbasis of positive \(X\), the inverse operator admits the integral representation \(Y=\int_0^\infty e^{-tX}(A+X^2)e^{-tX}\,dt\). Differentiate the integrand and integrate over the half line to verify the equation; the exponential vanishes at infinity. Each integrand is positive definite for finite \(t\). Thus an exact step from positive \(X\) yields positive \(Y\). This is specific to the equation and branch presently being solved.

Use a residual, a branch check and an independent solver

Run the notebook with target eigenvalues nine and one and a starting tilt of 35 degrees. The desired standard-deviation stretches are three and one. The plotted circle has radius one in input coordinates; the output contour has those semiaxes only for the target factor. A unit-radius contour in two dimensions is not a statement that 68 per cent of samples are enclosed. That probability requires a specified distribution and dimension. Read the ellipse here as a drawing of the linear transformation.

Set the initial angle to zero and repeat the comparison between the full and scalar-style derivatives. They now agree because \(X\) and \(A\) share eigenvectors. Next switch back to the tilted start and increase the target anisotropy. Observe the size of the first correction and the number of accepted steps. At the final iterate compare its square with the target and check its smallest eigenvalue. A negative definite square root could have residual zero as well. The sign constraint is therefore informative even for a solver that seems to have converged perfectly.

The optional OneSimulation calculation in the notebook takes an independent route through the derivative. Stacking the columns of \(H\) converts the Sylvester map into \(I\otimes X+X^T\otimes I\). A dense linear solve of the resulting four-by-four system should reproduce the rotated entrywise correction. Stacking rows while building the column-stacking operator would generally be a mistake. Large matrices would make the explicit Kronecker matrix expensive; the small example is valuable because it lets us compare two independently constructed maps directly.

Predict the effect of a small covariance error

The same derivative describes uncertainty in the result. If a measured target changes by a small symmetric matrix \(E\) and the root changes from \(S\) to \(S+\Delta\), expand the defining equation to obtain

\[S\Delta+\Delta S=E+O(\|\Delta\|^2).\]

Errors in the target's small-eigenvalue direction have a comparatively large effect on the fitted stretch there. For target eigenvalues nine and one, a diagonal covariance error of 0.06 produces first-order stretch errors of 0.01 or 0.03, depending on its direction. The relative errors differ by an additional factor of three because the original stretches are three and one.

An off-diagonal perturbation has a different interpretation. In the target eigenbasis its leading root entry is divided by the sum of the two stretches, here four. It changes the orientation of the ellipse at first order while leaving the axis lengths unchanged at that order for distinct eigenvalues. The distinction explains how visually similar residual norms can imply rather different changes to a calibrated instrument. It also shows what to record when comparing two repeat measurements: the directions of their errors should accompany the overall covariance norm.

Open editable notebook Download .ipynb

Tutorial on branches, scaling and derivatives

1. Take \(A=\operatorname{diag}(4,9)\). Give two distinct symmetric roots and identify which one the stated calibration selects.

Work through the solution

Both \(\operatorname{diag}(2,3)\) and \(\operatorname{diag}(-2,3)\) square to \(A\). Only the first is positive definite. The residual cannot distinguish them; a test of the eigenvalues or a positive-branch construction is needed.

2. At \(X=\operatorname{diag}(3,0.2)\), compare corrections produced by equal residual entries of magnitude 0.01 on each diagonal.

Work through the solution

The first diagonal correction has magnitude \(0.01/6\simeq0.00167\). The second has magnitude \(0.01/0.4=0.025\). Thus identical absolute covariance errors require corrections differing by a factor of fifteen in the two stretch directions.

3. In the tilted hand example, what is the predicted first off-diagonal entry of the updated nonlinear residual? How would one use it to distinguish a failed linear solve from an ordinary Newton truncation?

Work through the solution

The correct \(H^2_{12}\) equals

\[1/12+1/6=1/4.\]

With an inexact solve, \(F(X+H)-H^2=F(X)+XH+HX\) equals the residual of the linearised equation. Its norm tests solver accuracy separately from the nonlinear term intentionally left after a Newton step.

Problem sheet 7 · Project · Locate contact and calibrate a misleading residual

Two surfaces touch tangentially. A height sensor measures gap rather than signed distance. Decide what rootfinding, residual checks and a second sensor can each certify.

Recover the collision of two mathematical roots

Near the contact, model the gap as \(g(x,p)=(x-1)^2-p\), with all variables scaled by their reference units. Sketch its zeros for \(p\) above and below zero. For \(p=0.01\) begin with \([0.95,1.2]\) and test whether a sign-changing bracket isolates a root.

Work through the solution

Real roots are \(1\pm\sqrt p\) for positive \(p\), a double root at one for \(p=0\), and none for negative \(p\). At 0.95 the residual is \(-0.0075\), while at 1.2 it is 0.03. The bracket contains only the upper root at 1.1. A wider interval with both endpoints outside the two roots can have equal endpoint signs even when it contains both.

Explore the related calculation with adjustable inputs. Use the equilibrium parabola to inspect sensitivity and the tangent-coordinate continuation step. Translate the state coordinate if the fold is centred at a different value here.

Calibrate a stopping tolerance

With \(p=0\) begin at 1.2. Take three ordinary Newton steps and one multiplicity-corrected step. A calibrated sensor reading places the nonnegative gap in \([0,10^{-6}]\). What location interval follows from that bound? Compare the slopes in the multiple-root experiment.

Work through the solution

The successive locations are 1.1, 1.05 and 1.025. Multiplying the first Newton correction by two lands exactly on 1 for this pure square. A gap of \(10^{-6}\) is compatible with a displacement of magnitude \(10^{-3}\). Supplying more digits in the root does not improve that data-based bound.

Audit an algorithm that always reports convergence

At \(p=-0.001\) a least-squares routine stops at \(x=1\) and reports zero gradient. Find the residual and explain the report. Use the trust-region model to distinguish a decreasing merit function from a good final fit.

Work through the solution

The residual is 0.001, and the merit gradient \(gg'\) vanishes because \(g'=0\). This is the best fit of this model to the infeasible contact target. The positive minimum is 1,000 times the \(10^{-6}\) gap tolerance used in the preceding test. Report a nonzero closest separation and the uncertainty rather than a located contact.

Choose an additional measurement

You may measure one signed local slope \(g_x\) with uncertainty 0.002, or repeat the same absolute gap measurement. Which option resolves the side of a nearby contact? Express the position uncertainty before combining measurements.

Work through the solution

\(g_x=2(x-1)\) changes sign through contact. Its stated uncertainty yields an uncertainty of 0.001 in the inferred \(x\). An interval for the signed slope that excludes zero resolves which side of contact the instrument occupies. If the measured magnitude is at most 0.002, both signs still fit within the uncertainty. Repeated absolute gaps can improve precision in distance magnitude while retaining the symmetry between left and right solutions.

Problem sheet 8 · Find a periodic orbit when the starting point has no preferred phase

A nonlinear oscillator returns to its starting point after one period. Writing that fact as a root equation leaves a neutral direction and several unrelated solutions. A solvable amplitude oscillator lets you construct, stabilise and independently check a shooting method.

Count the unknowns in a periodic return

Consider the autonomous planar oscillator \(\dot x=(\mu-r^2)x-\omega y,\ \dot y=(\mu-r^2)y+\omega x,\ r^2=x^2+y^2\), with \(\omega>0\). The example uses scaled amplitudes and seconds. Let \(\Phi_T(u)\) denote the state after integrating for time \(T\) from \(u=(x,y)\). A shooting residual is

\[F(u,T)=\Phi_T(u)-u.\]

Determine what could go wrong if a three-component unknown \((x,y,T)\) is handed to a root solver with only these two residuals.

Recover the orbit and identify spurious roots

Use polar variables away from the origin to predict which residual zeros correspond to a single nontrivial revolution.

Work through the solution

Taking \(x\dot x+y\dot y\) gives

\[r\dot r=(\mu-r^2)r^2.\]

The angular numerator \(x\dot y-y\dot x=\omega r^2\) gives

\[\dot\theta=\omega.\]

For \(\mu>0\), \(r_*=\sqrt\mu\) is stationary and the period is

\[T_*=2\pi/\omega.\]

Every phase on the circle gives the same periodic orbit, so there is one redundant physical direction. Furthermore \(T=0\) gives zero residual for every point; the origin gives zero residual for every \(T\); and each integer multiple of \(T_*\) solves the return condition on the nonzero circle. A physically informative solve needs an initial estimate in the basin of the desired one-turn branch, positive period and a section choosing a phase. A tiny residual alone discriminates none of these cases.

Explore the related calculation with adjustable inputs. Change the parameters in the experiment on this same project page and compare its output with this part of the derivation.

Differentiate through a complete return

Border the Newton equations with a transverse section

Take \(g(u)=y=0\) with \(x>0\). Derive a Newton matrix including period as an unknown. Why would fixing \(x=\sqrt\mu\) be a bad phase condition at this same starting point?

Work through the solution

Let \(M=D_u\Phi_T(u)\) be the fundamental matrix propagated along the trajectory. Its columns solve \(\dot M=Df(\Phi_t(u))M,\ M(0)=I\). The derivative with respect to final time is the vector field evaluated at the end of the integration. Thus the linearised return and section equations are

\[\begin{pmatrix}M-I& f(\Phi_T(u))\\(0,1)&0\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\delta u\\\delta T\end{pmatrix}=-\begin{pmatrix}\Phi_T(u)-u\\y\end{pmatrix}.\]

At a nontrivial periodic point the vector \(f(u)\) is an eigenvector of \(M\) with eigenvalue one, because starting a little later along the same orbit merely shifts the final point along it. This explains the singularity of \(M-I\) before bordering. At \(u=(\sqrt\mu,0)\) the tangent points along positive \(y\), so

\[\nabla g\cdot f=\omega\sqrt\mu\ne0.\]

A section fixing \(x=\sqrt\mu\) is tangent to the circle there; its gradient annihilates \(f\). The new row then fails to remove phase degeneracy to first order even though it seems geometrically to locate the rightmost point.

Explore the related calculation with adjustable inputs. Change the parameters in the experiment on this same project page and compare its output with this part of the derivation.

Check stability and conditioning with an exact return map

Compute the transverse multiplier without an eigensolver

Put \(q=1/r^2\) and solve its scalar initial-value problem for \(r_0>0,\mu>0\). Use the result to check one period of numerical propagation for \(\mu=0.1,\ \omega=2\pi\).

Work through the solution

Differentiating gives \(\dot q=2-2\mu q\), hence \(q(T)=1/\mu+(q_0-1/\mu)e^{-2\mu T}\). The return radius on the positive axis after \(T_*\) is therefore

\[P(r_0)=\left[\frac{1-e^{-2\mu T_*}}{\mu}+\frac{e^{-2\mu T_*}}{r_0^2}\right]^{-1/2}.\]

At the fixed radius \(P'(r_*)=e^{-2\mu T_*}\). This also follows by linearising \(\dot r=\mu r-r^3\), whose derivative is \(-2\mu\) at \(r_*\). In the numerical case \(T_*=1.00\) s, \(r_*=0.31623\) and the transverse multiplier is \(0.81873\). The radial shooting residual has slope \(-0.18127\). A radial residual error of \(10^{-5}\) corresponds locally to a radius error of about \(5.52\times10^{-5}\). Near onset, the multiplier approaches unity and the phase condition alone cannot cure the true loss of transverse conditioning. For \(\mu\le0\) the positive finite-amplitude cycle ceases to exist, even though the origin remains an exact residual zero.

Explore the related calculation with adjustable inputs. Change the parameters in the experiment on this same project page and compare its output with this part of the derivation.

Separate time-integration error from the nonlinear residual

The experiment plots the signed one-revolution radial residual for many initial radii. One curve propagates the vector oscillator using classical fourth-order Runge–Kutta (RK4) in Cartesian coordinates. The other uses the analytical radial return. A sign change gives a candidate amplitude. The phase drift at that approximate root, reported separately, catches angular error that comparing final and initial radii alone would miss. Predict how both diagnostics should change when the time steps per turn double.

Open this experiment online

Choose a convergence test that excludes an equilibrium and a multiple turn

Describe a validation at \(\mu=0.1\) and design a check for a solver that accidentally reports \(T\simeq2\) seconds instead of one.

Work through the solution

Begin on a positive transverse section with radius near 0.316 and period near one second. Impose a positive radius floor only when selecting the intended branch, and check the integrated speed or path length to exclude a stationary solution. Resolve the radial residual with several integration meshes, testing that both return radius and phase error converge. A Newton iteration can drive the residual associated with a coarse integrator nearly to zero while its orbit is still inaccurate. Make integration error appreciably smaller than the desired orbit error after amplification by the near-singular transverse slope.

To check multiplicity, unwrap the trajectory angle or count positive-direction section crossings over the proposed orbit. The desired orbit accumulates \(2\pi\) and returns once; a two-turn solve accumulates \(4\pi\) and its nontrivial multiplier is \(e^{-4\mu T_*}=0.67032\) in this example. Stability can still appear physically sensible for a two-turn return. Counting turns or measuring a shorter repeated return tests something the residual and stability sign cannot. For systems without an analytic polar reduction, an event-defined Poincaré map and independently propagated variational equations provide the corresponding diagnostics.

Notebook practice · Newton's method for an unknown covariance square root

Nonlinear systems and iterative methods

Begin with a residual and ask which solution is accessible. Study how curvature, scaling and conditioning affect a computed root.

  1. Choosing a derivative step in a nonlinear solve

    A smaller finite-difference step eventually makes Newton iterations worse. Derive truncation and evaluation errors, compare a complex perturbation and a directional derivative, and test which numerical derivative is meaningful for the actual residual implementation.

  2. Accelerating fixed points with differences of residuals

    A slowly convergent physical iteration contains information in the direction of successive errors. Use a scalar extrapolation and a small residual-minimisation problem to exploit that information, then test the extrapolated state against the original equation.

  3. Finding another equilibrium after one root is known

    Newton iteration repeatedly finds the same structural equilibrium. Derive how deflation changes its basins while preserving other solutions, use a rank-one Jacobian update, and distinguish a list of discovered equilibria from a completeness certificate.

  4. Count enclosed zeros with a contour before locating them

    A complex residual may have several nearby zeros whose Newton basins are hard to distinguish. Use the argument principle to count them, contour moments to estimate their locations and an independent perturbation test to certify a root cluster.

  5. Where does a complex Newton step take its starting point?

    A single cubic has three equally simple roots. Derive the iteration, colour starting values by destination and use a separate convergence history to distinguish a bad first step from slow local convergence.

  6. Certifying a Newton step before the root is known

    A small correction is meaningful only together with a curvature estimate. Construct a ball that contains a solution and use a frozen linear solve to make damping decisions independent of equation units.

  7. Which correction does an experiment identify?

    Separate parameter combinations visible in a nonlinear fit, pass a regular fold by continuation and decide when to refine a boundary-value mesh before trusting its Newton residual.

Nonlinear systems and iterative methods · 1. Choosing a derivative step in a nonlinear solve

A smaller finite-difference step eventually makes Newton iterations worse. Derive truncation and evaluation errors, compare a complex perturbation and a directional derivative, and test which numerical derivative is meaningful for the actual residual implementation.

Derive competing contributions to error

Suppose \(f\) is smooth and you need its slope at \(x\) to form a Newton correction. A forward difference \(D_h=[f(x+h)-f(x)]/h\) has Taylor expansion \(f'(x)+hf''(x)/2+O(h^2)\). A centred difference \([f(x+h)-f(x-h)]/(2h)\) instead cancels the even terms, leaving \(f'(x)+h^2 f'''(x)/6+O(h^4)\). Both limits are exact in real arithmetic as \(h\to0\). The program, however, returns approximate residual values, and a subtraction of nearby values can lose significant digits. If each absolute evaluation error is at most \(\delta\), the derivative error from those perturbations is at most \(2\delta/h\) for forward difference and \(\delta/h\) for the centred one.

Thus an illustrative forward error bound has shape \(a h+b/h\); minimisation gives

\[h_*=\sqrt{b/a}.\]

The centred bound \(a h^2+b/h\) has minimiser \(h_*=(b/(2a))^{1/3}\). Floating-point roundoff acting on \(O(1)\) residual values often gives the familiar square-root and cube-root machine-precision scales, but the physical units of \(x\), the scale of the derivative and noise from the residual solver must be included. A nonlinear function evaluated by an iterative PDE solve can have a much larger \(\delta\) than roundoff. Repeating the inner solve with tighter tolerance can shift the optimal step considerably.

For \(f(x)=e^x\) at zero, the forward leading error is \(h/2\), centred error \(h^2/6\). If \(\delta=10^{-10}\) as an absolute per-value uncertainty, a forward worst-case bound gives optimal \(h=2\times10^{-5}\), while the centred model gives

\[h=(3\times10^{-10})^{1/3}=6.69\times10^{-4}.\]

The centred step can be much larger yet retain lower truncation error. This is a scale estimate using worst-case errors, not a guarantee about the phase or stochastic correlation of actual errors. If queries share common random numbers or a warm-started solver, errors can partly cancel or acquire a systematic derivative bias.

Obtain the slope from an imaginary perturbation

For a real-valued function with an analytic complex extension near the real argument, expand \(f(x+ih)=f(x)+ihf'(x)-h^2f''(x)/2-ih^3f'''(x)/6+\cdots\). Taking its imaginary part and dividing by \(h\) gives second-order accuracy with no explicit subtraction of two nearly equal real numbers. For \(e^x\) at zero, the result is \(\sin h/h\). At very small representable \(h\) the imaginary signal is itself proportional to \(h\) and may still be carried accurately by analytic elementary operations. This advantage disappears if the computation discards imaginary parts, uses nonanalytic absolute values, branches on the perturbation, or applies a black-box routine using complex arithmetic for unrelated purposes. Automatic differentiation supplies another route through differentiable implemented operations, but it too needs the program's mathematical definition to match the derivative being requested.

Audit a Jacobian action before assembling a matrix

For \(F:\mathbb R^n\to\mathbb R^m\), pick direction \(v\) and examine \(R(h)=\|F(x+hv)-F(x)-hJv\|\). If a proposed Jacobian action is correct and \(F\) is twice differentiable, \(R(h)\) initially scales like \(h^2\). A wrong first derivative generally produces a leading \(O(h)\) term. The test checks orientation, units, signs and chain rules without constructing a dense finite-difference matrix. Test several directions and scales; a wrong matrix can annihilate the one chosen direction. At very small \(h\) a roundoff or inner-solver floor eventually hides both ideal slopes. When working with constraints use perturbations remaining inside the physical domain or give derivatives in a local feasible parametrisation.

Find the useful step before a Newton update

The experiment evaluates the forward, centred and complex-step slopes of a smooth elementary residual in floating-point arithmetic. It plots absolute errors on logarithmic scales and compares them with the exact derivative. Move the evaluation point and note that occasional accidental zeros are rounded at the plotting floor. Their exact location is not a robust guarantee for a different residual. Compare the range of steps with a reproducible error trend before interpreting one apparently perfect result.

Open this experiment online

Change the evaluation before changing the step

For the exponential the forward numerator has an exact factorisation \(e^{x+h}-e^x=e^x(e^h-1)\). A numerical library's expm1(h) evaluates the last factor accurately even for very small \(h\), using a local series or a reduced-argument algorithm. Form the quotient as \(e^x\,\mathrm{expm1}(h)/h\). Its mathematical truncation error is still \(e^xh/2+O(h^2)\). Its rounding behaviour differs because the two terms near \(e^x\) are never computed separately. At \(x=1\) and \(h=10^{-18}\), adding \(h\) to a binary64 value of one rounds back to one. The direct forward quotient then returns zero. The factored evaluation gives the derivative \(e\) to working precision.

This experiment diagnoses the source of an error curve. If the algebraic form is inaccessible, a finite-difference checker sees black-box function values and cannot apply the factorisation. If the entire residual is available, stable elementary functions can change the derivative accuracy of a Newton step without changing its formal order.

Tutorial · design tests for smoothness and implementation errors

Diagnose a forward-difference Newton iteration

For \(f(x)=x^2-2\) at \(x=1.4\), obtain the exact dependence of a forward derivative estimate on \(h\). Compare one Newton step using \(h=0.1\) with the exact Jacobian.

Work through the solution

The difference quotient is

\[2x+h=2.8+h.\]

The residual is \(-0.04\). The exact step gives

\[x_{\rm new}=1.4+0.04/2.8=1.414286;\]

using 0.1 gives \(1.413793\). Centred differences evaluate this polynomial's derivative exactly in real arithmetic for every nonzero \(h\), but roundoff can spoil very small steps. The discrepancy at large \(h\) is a calculable systematic truncation, so merely tightening the outer Newton stopping tolerance cannot remove it.

Catch a direction missed by one test

A proposed Jacobian differs from the correct one by \(E=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&-1\\0&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)\). Compare tests along \((1,1)\) and \((1,0)\) and show which observed slope would be misleading.

Work through the solution

The first direction lies in the nullspace of \(E\), so its Taylor remainder can show the expected \(h^2\) slope despite an incorrect Jacobian. The second has \(Ev=(1,0)\), producing an \(O(h)\) defect. Random directions augment a set of unit- or physics-informed probes, particularly when an error affects a single column or constraint block. One successful slope does not establish correctness of an entire matrix.

Determine whether complex step applies to a norm

A residual returns \(f(x)=|x|\) using a complex modulus routine. At \(x=1\), what does a complex-step call produce? Is this evidence that the real function has zero derivative there?

Work through the solution

The routine computes \(|1+ih|=\sqrt{1+h^2}\), purely real, so the reported complex-step slope is zero. The real derivative at \(x=1\) equals one. The implementation used a nonanalytic map of a complex variable. At a known positive branch the analytic local extension \(f(z)=z\) gives the correct derivative, while the real function at the kink \(x=0\) has no classical derivative. Check both the real regularity and what complex arithmetic actually executes before applying this technique.

Nonlinear systems and iterative methods · 2. Accelerating fixed points with differences of residuals

A slowly convergent physical iteration contains information in the direction of successive errors. Use a scalar extrapolation and a small residual-minimisation problem to exploit that information, then test the extrapolated state against the original equation.

Eliminate an unknown leading error without knowing the root

Suppose a sequence obeys \(x_j=x_*+c q^j\) for an unknown limit and mode multiplier \(q\ne1\). Subtract successive terms to find

\[\Delta x_{j+1}/\Delta x_j=q.\]

Eliminating \(q,c\) yields the Aitken extrapolation \(\hat x_j=x_j-(x_{j+1}-x_j)^2/(x_{j+2}-2x_{j+1}+x_j)\). For a single exact geometric error it returns the limit after observing three values, even if the multiplier is negative and the iterates oscillate. If the denominator is near zero relative to numerical uncertainty, the extrapolation can amplify noise. A nearly constant first difference may indicate almost linear drift rather than a trustworthy asymptotic geometric error.

For \(g(x)=0.95x+0.10\), ordinary iteration from zero gives \(0,0.10,0.195\); the fixed point is 2. The second difference is \(-0.005\) and the formula gives exactly 2. If the last reported value is rounded to 0.196, the denominator changes to \(-0.004\), giving an extrapolated 2.50. Substituting this candidate into the original residual \(g(x)-x\) gives \(-0.025\): it is a worse fixed point than 2 but still has a modest residual because the fixed point itself is weakly conditioned. Always evaluate the actual residual and estimate its relation to state error if a meaningful physical tolerance is required.

Choose affine weights that minimise residual history

For a vector fixed-point map \(g\), let \(f_i=g(x_i)-x_i\). Seek a combination of recent residuals with small norm while preserving a fixed point under averaging. Anderson mixing chooses coefficients \(\alpha_i\) minimising \(\|\sum_i\alpha_i f_i\|\) subject to \(\sum_i\alpha_i=1\), then evaluates a candidate \(x_{\rm new}=\sum_i\alpha_i g(x_i)\) in a basic undamped formulation. The affine constraint allows negative coefficients, which is precisely what makes acceleration beyond ordinary averaging possible. For one-dimensional affine maps the combination can eliminate a single mode exactly.

For two residual vectors write their affine combination as \(f_1+\beta(f_0-f_1)\). Its least-squares optimum is \(\beta=-f_1^T(f_0-f_1)/\|f_0-f_1\|^2\) if the denominator is nonzero. With several vectors a small QR or SVD solve in terms of differences is preferable to building normal equations when differences are nearly collinear. Choose component units or a weighted norm consistent with the physical equation. Otherwise a residual expressed in large numerical units can dominate the fit and accelerate one variable while neglecting another important constraint.

For a strictly affine map \(g(x)=Gx+c\),

\[f(\sum\alpha_i x_i)=\sum\alpha_i f_i.\]

The predicted residual at the affine combination of old states is exactly the linear combination. The candidate formed by applying \(g\) to it has residual \(G\sum\alpha_i f_i\). For nonlinear \(g\) neither equality need hold globally. An Anderson residual minimisation is a local extrapolation hypothesis, not a proof that the next nonlinear state decreases residual or stays feasible. Evaluating \(f(x_{\rm new})\), accepting a damped mixture when appropriate, and dropping near-dependent history are practical ways to test that hypothesis.

Background · What happens when two residual histories almost coincide?

Take two residuals from nearly consecutive states and minimise the norm of \(r_1+\alpha(r_2-r_1)\). Write \(d=r_2-r_1\). Differentiating the squared norm with respect to \(\alpha\) gives

\[\alpha=-\frac{d^T r_1}{d^T d}.\]

The denominator vanishes for duplicate residuals. A tiny denominator can also demand enormous positive and negative weights that cancel in the modelled residual. For scalar residuals 1 and 0.999 those weights are about \(-999\) and \(1000\). Extrapolating the nonlinear states this far relies on local behaviour far outside the sampled interval. One practical response is to discard a nearly redundant column, then evaluate the true residual of the proposed state. A small residual of the affine model alone provides no acceptance test.

Separate fast and slow modes in a two-component relaxation

Use \(g(x,y)=(0.9x+0.1,0.2y+0.8)\), whose fixed point is \((1,1)\). Start at \((0,0)\), then \(x_1=(0.1,0.8)\). Residuals are \(f_0=(0.1,0.8)\) and \(f_1=(0.09,0.16)\); their difference is \((0.01,0.64)\). The two-history coefficient above is about \(-0.252\). The predicted residual of the affine state is approximately \((0.0875,-0.0010)\), largely eliminating the fast second component while leaving the slower first. Three appropriately independent histories can eliminate two geometrically distinct error directions for this affine example if their residual vectors affinely span zero in exact arithmetic. Near-equal decay factors can make that calculation badly conditioned. A finite memory window trades acceleration against conditioning, storage and the cost of residual evaluation.

Follow a two-mode residual through a real mixing step

Set the slow multiplier to 0.90 and the second to 0.20. The first two ordinary states coincide in both runs because the accelerated iteration has no history at the start. Thereafter the calculator solves the two-vector minimisation at each iteration, forms the proposed mixed state and evaluates its actual residual. The second component initially dominates the residual norm even though it is the more rapidly damped mode. Notice how the mixed and plain curves separate as the slow component becomes limiting. The plotted logarithmic residual measures convergence to the stated map; the physical state error in mode j is its residual divided by the magnitude of one minus that mode multiplier.

Move the two multipliers close together so the map becomes nearly a scalar relaxation, then choose a negative second multiplier so its errors alternate. Explain why affine mixing can nearly remove a single geometric mode, while nearly dependent histories make weights sensitive to evaluation noise. The implementation falls back to the ordinary map if the difference norm is already at the roundoff threshold. Very small residuals are marked at a finite plotting floor. Compare curves before that floor rather than fitting a convergence rate to the flat portion.

Tutorial · identify good and bad extrapolation data

Check an alternating scalar series

Three iterates are 1.50, 0.75 and 1.125. Infer the geometric multiplier and limit. How should the next value be checked?

Work through the solution

The differences are \(-0.75\) and \(+0.375\), so \(q=-0.5\). The Aitken prediction is

\[1.50-0.75^2/1.125=1.00.\]

If the geometric assumption persists, the next raw iterate would be \(1-0.0625=0.9375\). Comparing both the new observed iterate and the actual residual at 1 tests the single-mode hypothesis. Three arbitrary numbers alone can always fit such a geometric extrapolation when the denominator is nonzero.

Show the connection to a secant root step

Use two scalar residuals \(f_i=g(x_i)-x_i\). Derive the affine combination of \(x_0,x_1\) whose predicted residual is zero and compare with a secant step for \(f=0\).

Work through the solution

Solve \(\alpha f_0+(1-\alpha)f_1=0\) to get

\[\alpha=-f_1/(f_0-f_1).\]

The mixed state is \(x_1-f_1(x_1-x_0)/(f_1-f_0)\), the ordinary secant prediction. The combination of \(g(x_i)\) is the same state because the weighted residual vanishes. For nearly equal residuals the denominator can be tiny, generating a large extrapolation. A bounded step or fallback to the base fixed-point map may be necessary if the candidate leaves the valid state domain.

Select a restart from nearly identical residual columns

Two stored vectors have residuals \((1,0)\) and \((1,10^{-9})\), measured with absolute component errors \(10^{-7}\). Can their difference support a reliable two-history fit?

Work through the solution

The direction being used for the second independent sample is smaller than the evaluation uncertainty by two orders of magnitude. Its orientation and length can be dominated by numerical error, so a large coefficient in a least-squares solve carries little physical information. Drop the redundant direction or use a rank-revealing solve scaled to the expected residual error. Evaluate any retained candidate on the original model. A tiny predicted mixed residual from cancelling inaccurate large coefficients has no independent evidential value.

Further reading

  • Walker and Ni · Anderson acceleration for fixed-point iterations
  • Course investigation · contractive maps and error bounds
  • Solve an implicit cooling step with a changing residual

Nonlinear systems and iterative methods · 3. Finding another equilibrium after one root is known

Newton iteration repeatedly finds the same structural equilibrium. Derive how deflation changes its basins while preserving other solutions, use a rank-one Jacobian update, and distinguish a list of discovered equilibria from a completeness certificate.

Prevent a known simple zero from attracting another iteration

Suppose a differentiable \(F:\mathbb R^n\to\mathbb R^n\) has a known zero \(r\), with nonsingular Jacobian \(J(r)\). Near \(r\), \(F(r+h)=J(r)h+O(\|h\|^2)\), so its norm is bounded below by a positive constant times \(\|h\|\) in a sufficiently small neighbourhood. Multiplying by a nonvanishing scalar multiplier away from \(r\) preserves all other zeros. Taking \(M(x)=\|x-r\|^{-p}+\alpha\), \(\alpha>0\), yields deflated residual \(G(x)=M(x)F(x)\) on \(x\ne r\). Its norm behaves at least as a constant times \(\|x-r\|^{1-p}\) near the simple root. For \(p=1\) it need not have a directional limit but cannot approach zero there; for \(p>1\) it diverges. The scalar shift \(\alpha\) prevents the multiplier from vanishing at large distances.

If the root has multiplicity \(m\) in a scalar problem, \(|F|\) can vanish like distance to the power \(m\). An exponent \(p<m\) can leave the deflated norm tending to zero, invalidating the reasoning for simple roots. In systems with singular Jacobians different directions can have different leading powers or a whole manifold of solutions can pass through \(r\). One should identify symmetries and solve a properly constrained problem before treating every coordinate representation of a continuum of roots as a new equilibrium. Deflation changes a nonlinear solver's route; it does not make a singular physical problem isolated by decree.

Reuse a Newton correction with a rank-one change

Differentiate the scalar product to obtain

\[J_G=MJ_F+F(\nabla M)^T.\]

When \(J_F\) is invertible, let \(d=-J_F^{-1}F\) be the ordinary Newton correction. Look for a deflated step \(s=\beta d\). Applying \(J_G\) gives \(\beta[-MF+F(\nabla M)^Td]\). Equating this to \(-MF\) yields

\[s=\frac{d}{1-(\nabla\log M)^Td}.\]

This is a convenient exact relation for a scalar deflation multiplier away from a vanishing denominator. The Jacobian of the modified problem can become singular at points where the original Newton step is perfectly well-defined. A denominator near zero can create an enormous proposed state change. A line search or trust region should verify the candidate against the chosen objective and the physically admissible variables. Once a different candidate root is located, evaluate the original \(F\), its Jacobian conditioning and any invariant constraints independently.

See the change of basin in an analytical scalar model

Take \(F(x)=x(x-1)(x+2)\) with roots \(0,1,-2\), and let \(r=0\) have already been found. On the positive half-line choose \(M=1/x\) for a transparent algebraic comparison, giving \(G=(x-1)(x+2)\). This unshifted local example works on this domain; a global implementation often uses the positive shifted norm expression instead. From \(x=0.2\),

\[F=-0.352,\ F'=3x^2+2x-2=-1.48.\]

Original Newton gives approximately \(-0.0378\), entering the basin of the known zero. Deflated Newton has \(G=-1.76,\ G'=1.4\) and gives approximately \(1.4571\), entering a region directed toward the other positive root. Neither update used knowledge of the other roots to choose that direction.

For a polynomial with known exact factor, explicit algebraic division may be available. A numerical residual from a mechanics or fluid solver need not be a polynomial and generally offers no such division. Numerical deflation uses the known state and a norm to remove its attraction while continuing to evaluate the same physical governing equations. Use physical scaling in that norm. The distance between two states measured in metres and kelvins is arbitrary until component scales are chosen; an apparently huge separation in the deflation metric can represent tiny physical changes. If roots are known only approximately, multiplying a near-singular factor around a slightly wrong centre can leave the true nearby root or create severe numerical conditioning. Refine and verify each original root before including it in a list of deflation centres.

Change the Newton basin by dividing out a known root

Here the original residual is the cubic in the worked example. Its known zero at the origin is algebraically divided out, leaving a quadratic on the full line. This exact-factor experiment isolates the change in Newton geometry without introducing a singular numerical evaluation of an inverse distance. For both residuals, the calculator starts at the same chosen positive point and applies the same damping factor. At the default 0.20 the first ordinary step goes slightly negative and approaches zero; the deflated step passes one and converges back to it. The readout verifies the second candidate in the original cubic.

Try a smaller damping factor and compare the number of iterations needed before the two candidate sequences settle. Damping moderates each proposed correction but does not restore the removed root of the quadratic. A finite iteration count with heavy damping may end with a visible original residual; a current candidate is not an exact equilibrium merely because its curve has begun to flatten. This illustration relies on an exact polynomial factor, while deflation around a numerically inferred state requires an explicit centre tolerance and original-model verification.

Tutorial · separate discovery, verification and completeness

Work through a simple shifted multiplier

For \(F=x(x-1)\), \(x>0\), use \(M=1+1/x\) to deflate zero. Find the new residual, its value as \(x\to0^+\), and the deflated Newton step from \(x=0.5\).

Work through the solution

Here \(G=(x+1)(x-1)=x^2-1\), tending to \(-1\) at the excluded zero. From \(x=0.5\), \(G=-0.75,\ G'=1\), giving \(x_{\rm next}=1.25\). The original derivative \(2x-1\) vanishes exactly at the starting point even though the deflated derivative does not. The shortcut requiring \(J_F^{-1}\) is unavailable there; directly differentiating and solving for \(G\) remains valid. This example shows why a formula derived for nonsingular \(J_F\) should not become a universal implementation assumption.

Choose a multiplier for a double root

For \(F(x)=(x-1)^2(x+1)\) deflate \(r=1\) with \(M=|x-1|^{-p}+1\). Compare \(p=1\) and \(p=2\). What limits a near-root termination test?

Work through the solution

Near \(1\), \(|F|\sim2|x-1|^2\). With \(p=1\), \(|G|\sim2|x-1|\) still tends to zero; with \(p=2\), it tends to two. A test accepting just a small deflated residual can therefore return very close to the old double root when using the inappropriate exponent. A small original residual also occurs over a relatively wide neighbourhood of a multiple root. Report a root-separation test and independent bounds or refinement where multiplicity affects the required state tolerance.

Construct a genuine completeness argument in one dimension

Several initial guesses and deflations recover the three roots of \(x^3+x^2-2x\). What additional reasoning certifies that the list is complete, and what can be reported if only a black-box smooth system is available?

Work through the solution

The polynomial is nonzero of degree three, so it has at most three distinct complex roots. Substitution verifies three distinct real ones and proves completeness in this case. Multiple Newton solves by themselves would not establish that count. For a smooth black-box scalar problem over a compact interval one can partition it, enclose the function and its derivative, exclude root-free pieces and isolate all retained candidates when the bounds allow. In a higher-dimensional problem a list found by starts and deflation should be reported as verified discovered roots unless an independent argument covers the entire specified domain.

Further reading

  • Farrell, Birkisson and Funke · deflation for distinct nonlinear solutions
  • Course investigation · continuing an equilibrium branch
