Nonlinear systems and iterative methods · 4. Count enclosed zeros with a contour before locating them

A complex residual may have several nearby zeros whose Newton basins are hard to distinguish. Use the argument principle to count them, contour moments to estimate their locations and an independent perturbation test to certify a root cluster.

Turn winding of a residual into a root count

Let \(f\) be analytic inside and on a positively oriented simple closed contour \(\Gamma\), with no zeros on it. Suppose \(z_j\) is an enclosed zero of multiplicity \(m_j\). Locally \(f(z)=(z-z_j)^{m_j}g(z)\) with \(g(z_j)\ne0\), so \(f'/f=m_j/(z-z_j)+g'/g\). Cauchy's residue theorem gives

\[N=\frac1{2\pi i}\oint_\Gamma\frac{f'(z)}{f(z)}\,dz=\sum_jm_j.\]

A meromorphic \(f\) instead gives number of zeros minus poles, counted with multiplicity. On the image curve \(f(\Gamma)\) the same integral equals its change of complex argument divided by \(2\pi\). This provides an alternative if only complex function evaluations are available. Both versions require that the contour avoid zeros and poles. Moving the contour through one changes its integer count discontinuously; a point extremely close to the boundary demands finer sampling or a different contour.

For a circle \(z=c+Re^{i\theta}\), periodic trapezoidal quadrature evaluates the integral as the mean of \((z-c)f'(z)/f(z)\). The smoothness and distance to nearby singularities govern convergence. Simply rounding the result to the nearest integer can disguise severe undersampling. Compare independent contour radii and doubled angular grids, inspect the minimum \(|f|\) on the boundary, and examine unwrapped phase increments. A grid with only endpoint samples of a rapidly rotating image can miss entire turns even when successive complex values look nearly identical. An upper bound on phase speed or adaptive interval enclosure is needed for a rigorous count from discrete samples.

Locate small groups after counting them

Multiply the integrand by \(z^k\). Its residues become \(m_j z_j^k\), giving power sums \(s_k=(2\pi i)^{-1}\oint z^k f'/f\,dz\). For exactly one simple enclosed root, \(s_1\) is its location. For two simple roots \(z_1,z_2\), obtain their sum \(s_1\), and their product \((s_1^2-s_2)/2\) from the identity \((z_1+z_2)^2=z_1^2+z_2^2+2z_1z_2\). Solve the resulting quadratic for initial guesses, then polish on the original residual and recheck the contour count. For large clusters reconstructing a high-degree polynomial from power sums can be ill-conditioned; recursively subdivide or use a contour method designed for stable extraction.

Take \(f(z)=(z-0.2)(z+0.4)(z-2)\) on the unit circle. Its count is two; its moments are

\[s_1=-0.2,\ s_2=0.20.\]

The reconstructed product is \((0.04-0.20)/2=-0.08\), giving \(z^2+0.2z-0.08=0\) and roots \(0.2,-0.4\). The exterior factor changes the residual values and Newton basins but contributes no interior pole of \(f'/f\), hence no moment. A contour enclosing all three instead changes the power sums. Severe cancellation can occur when several large root powers sum to a small number. Shift and scale coordinates about the contour centre before evaluating high-order moments.

Use a boundary inequality to protect a count under perturbation

If an analytically tractable \(g\) and target \(f=g+h\) satisfy \(|h(z)|<|g(z)|\) on \(\Gamma\), then the continuous homotopy \(g+th,\ 0\le t\le1\), has no boundary zero. Its winding number around zero cannot change continuously between distinct integers, so \(f\) and \(g\) enclose the same number of zeros. This is Rouché's theorem, useful when a perturbation is small on the boundary even if it strongly splits individual roots inside. The strict bound must cover the entire curve. Observations at a handful of sample points without a bound between them are numerical evidence, not a theorem.

For \(f(z)=(z-1)^2+\epsilon z,\ |\epsilon|=0.001\), use a circle of radius 0.1 about one. Its unperturbed modulus is 0.01 and the perturbation has magnitude at most 0.0011. Both zeros stay inside this circle. Their individual shifts can be of order \(\sqrt{|\epsilon|}\) because the reference zero is double. A small coefficient perturbation can therefore leave a reliably countable cluster while making its individual members comparatively sensitive. This distinction between a cluster as a whole and a single ill-conditioned root is important when a model residual depends on uncertain fitted coefficients.

Count one movable complex zero by discrete contour integration

The experiment integrates the logarithmic derivative of a polynomial with one zero strictly inside the unit circle. Its exact count is therefore one everywhere in the slider range. The real and imaginary parts of the quadrature estimate are plotted separately against the number of uniformly spaced angular samples. At radius 0.9 on the positive real axis, eight samples give about 1.756; rounding the real part would report two zeros. Moving the root farther from the contour makes convergence faster without changing the true count.

Rotate the root slightly while keeping its radius fixed. The absolute distance from the contour is unchanged, but its phase relative to the sampling nodes changes. A nonzero imaginary estimate and oscillatory errors may appear even though the exact winding number is real. The finite-grid formula for a root at complex a is one divided by one minus a raised to the number of samples. Use it to predict locations of large errors before reading the graph. An apparent agreement for one particular sample count can be accidental; compare successive refinements and the complex error.

Open this experiment online

Tutorial · audit a contour computation

Calculate the exact aliasing of a one-root count

For \(f(z)=z-a\) on the unit circle with \(|a|<1\), use \(M\) equally spaced points. Derive the trapezoidal estimate of \(N\) and evaluate it for real \(a=0.9,M=8\).

Work through the solution

At each point \(zf'/f=1/(1-a/z)=\sum_{k\ge0}a^kz^{-k}\). Averaging roots of unity cancels all terms except powers divisible by \(M\), giving \(N_M=1/(1-a^M)\). At \(M=8\) it is about 1.756, which would round to the wrong integer two. Increasing to \(M=64\) gives about 1.00118. This explicit example illustrates why apparent smoothness of the original analytic function does not ensure adequate sampling of its logarithmic derivative.

Recover a repeated root from moments

A contour yields \(N=2,s_1=1.2,s_2=0.72\) to high numerical accuracy. Infer the candidate roots and explain the sensitivity to a small error in \(s_2\).

Work through the solution

The product is \((1.44-0.72)/2=0.36\); the polynomial \(z^2-1.2z+0.36\) has double root 0.6. A change \(\delta\) in \(s_2\) changes the product by \(-\delta/2\), giving inferred splitting \(\pm\sqrt{\delta/2}\) relative to 0.6. Tiny moment noise can therefore mimic two distinct roots. Verify a multiplicity claim against derivative information on the original function and clarify whether distinct roots can actually be resolved.

Explain a zero net winding that hides a zero

A rational residual has a simple zero at 0.1 and a simple pole at -0.2 inside the unit circle. What would the count report and how could one recover the desired zero count?

Work through the solution

The logarithmic derivative has residues plus one and minus one, so net winding is zero even though a zero exists. If the pole and its order are known, add the pole count to the measured winding or multiply by a known denominator that removes it and then check the resulting analytic function. A near pole–zero cancellation can be difficult to resolve numerically. Treating a rational or discretely singular residual as a globally analytic polynomial would make the count's interpretation incorrect.

  • ETH Zürich · numerical methods for CSE, polynomial roots and nonlinear systems

Nonlinear systems and iterative methods · 5. Where does a complex Newton step take its starting point?

A single cubic has three equally simple roots. Derive the iteration, colour starting values by destination and use a separate convergence history to distinguish a bad first step from slow local convergence.

Continue the tangent step into the complex plane

A holomorphic function has a complex linear approximation \(f(z+s)\approx f(z)+f'(z)s\). Requiring this approximation to vanish gives \(s=-f(z)/f'(z)\) wherever the derivative is nonzero. For \(f(z)=z^3-1\), the resulting map is

\[N(z)=\frac{2z}{3}+\frac{1}{3z^2}.\]

The second term is a pole at zero. Starting at a small positive number sends the next iterate far to the right, after which large iterates return approximately by multiplication by 2/3. At a root \(r\), write \(z=r+e\) and substitute \(r^3=1\). Exact cancellation of the linear error gives

\[N(r+e)-r=e^2(3r+2e)/[3(r+e)^2].\]

Sufficiently near \(r\), the new error is about \(e^2/r\). This argument predicts the speed after entry into a local neighbourhood. It has no information about which root a distant initial point will reach.

Let \(s=\exp(2\pi i/3)\). Since \(s^3=1\), symmetry gives

\[N(sz)=sN(z).\]

The entire sequence, including its root, rotates with the start. Conjugate starting points likewise produce conjugate histories because the coefficients are real. These are two independent checks for numerical basin maps. We classify a pixel only once its residual falls below a declared tolerance. A starting pole and a trajectory that exhausts its iteration allowance can both appear unclassified. Increasing the allowance separates these cases for many pixels.

Follow a detour before quadratic convergence

From \(z_0=1/2\) the first iterate is \(1/3+4/3=5/3\). The next is \(10/9+3/25=277/225\approx1.23111\). The error initially rises from 0.5 to 0.667. By contrast, starting at 0.1 gives \(z_1=33.4\). The subsequent near-geometric return explains the long shoulder in the second figure. Both starts ultimately approach the real root.

Move the orange sample by writing a complex number such as \(-0.5+0.85i\) in Python as -0.5+0.85j. The sample mark and the residual trace follow the same new starting point. The intricate boundaries in the image are computed from independent point trajectories. Refining the grid examines finer samples of those boundaries; the speed on the residual graph belongs to one particular start.

Exercise · obtain two basin colours for free

Suppose \(0.75+0.10i\) reaches the real root in five updates. What are the destinations and update counts after multiplying the start by either non-real cube root of one?

Work through the solution

Write the original iterates as \(z_k\). By induction \(N^k(sz_0)=sz_k\) and \(N^k(s^2z_0)=s^2z_k\). The destinations are \(s\) and \(s^2\). Since \((sz_k)^3-1=z_k^3-1\), every residual and hence the first crossing of the same threshold is unchanged. Differences in a finely sampled plot may arise because the rotated point falls between pixels.

Exercise · quantify the final two digits

For a real iterate \(z=1.01\), estimate the next error by the quadratic approximation and calculate its exact value using the error formula.

Work through the solution

The leading term gives \(0.01^2=10^{-4}\). The exact error is \(10^{-4}(3.02)/(3\times1.01^2)\approx9.8683\times10^{-5}\). The difference is of the next order in the initial error. The small residual after this update is about three times the error because \(f'(1)=3\).

Nonlinear systems and iterative methods · 6. Certifying a Newton step before the root is known

A small correction is meaningful only together with a curvature estimate. Construct a ball that contains a solution and use a frozen linear solve to make damping decisions independent of equation units.

Bound all future corrections from initial data

Let \(F\) be differentiable on a convex neighbourhood, \(J_0=F'(x_0)\) invertible, and fix an induced norm. Suppose \(\|J_0^{-1}F(x_0)\|\le b\) and \(\|J_0^{-1}(F'(x)-F'(y))\|\le L\|x-y\|\). These estimates must hold on the entire proposed ball. Define the scalar majorant \(g(t)=b-t+Lt^2/2\). If \(2Lb<1\), its smaller zero is \(r=2b/(1+\sqrt{1-2Lb})\). Require the closed ball of that radius to lie in the domain.

Set \(t_0=0\) and apply scalar Newton to \(g\). Since \(g\) is decreasing and convex on \([0,r]\), the iterates increase to \(r\). At a point no farther than \(t\) from \(x_0\), the Neumann series for \(J_0^{-1}F'(x)=I+E\), \(\|E\|\le Lt<1\), bounds \(\|F'(x)^{-1}J_0\|\le 1/(1-Lt)\). If \(s\) is an exact Newton correction, the constant and linear terms cancel. Integrating the Jacobian variation along that step gives

\[\|J_0^{-1}F(x+s)\|\le L\|s\|^2/2.\]

At the scalar Newton points exactly the same formula holds with equality for \(g(t_{j+1})=L(t_{j+1}-t_j)^2/2\). Induction now gives \(\|x_{j+1}-x_j\|\le t_{j+1}-t_j\). The vector sequence is Cauchy and stays in the ball, its limit is a root and \(\|x_*-x_j\|\le r-t_j\). Two roots in that ball would be joined by an averaged Jacobian differing from the identity, after scaling, by at most \(Lr<1\). It is invertible, so the roots coincide.

Use the linearised correction as the measuring stick

Premultiply the equations by any fixed nonsingular matrix \(S\). The Newton correction and the majorant data stay the same since \((SJ_0)^{-1}S=J_0^{-1}\). The Euclidean norm of the raw residual generally changes. For a trial factor \(\lambda\), form \(s=-J^{-1}F(x)\) and \(\bar s=-J^{-1}F(x+\lambda s)\) with the same factorisation. Linear prediction gives \(\bar s=(1-\lambda)s+e\), where local curvature bounded in the current scaled norm implies \(\|e\|\le L_x\lambda^2\|s\|^2/2\). Backtrack until \(\|e\|\le \lambda\|s\|/2\). Then \(\|\bar s\|\le(1-\lambda/2)\|s\|\). For smooth regular points sufficiently small steps pass. Rebuild the Jacobian after acceptance. Global convergence requires additional bounds keeping iterates within a regular level set.

For \(F=(x_1^2-2,x_2^2-1)\) start at \((1.5,0.9)\) in the maximum norm. Here \(b=0.19/1.8\) and \(L=\max(1/1.5,1/0.9)\). Both follow directly from the diagonal Jacobian. With \(2Lb<1\) the positive quadrant contains the certified ball.

OneSimulation experiment · Test the radius after a change of units

Replace a starting coordinate by a smaller positive value. Recheck the certificate before iterating.

Download the executable notebook · Python source

Adjust the experiment in the browser. Change the two starting coordinates for \(F=(x_1^2-2,x_2^2-1)\). The plot recomputes the iterates, the maximum-norm correction \(\beta\), the preconditioned derivative bound \(L=\max(1/x_{1,0},1/x_{2,0})\), and the guaranteed radius whenever \(\beta L\le1/2\). Move beyond the certified region and observe that failure of this sufficient test does not establish divergence. Rescale the first equation by up to six orders of magnitude: its Jacobian must receive the same scale. The browser uses the explicit diagonal solve; the notebook performs the corresponding OneSimulation dense solve.

Open the live experiment

"""Test a Newton certificate without knowing the solution in advance."""
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
import numpy as np
from onesim.numerics.linear.matrices.dense_linear_algebra import solve_dense

start = np.array([1.5, 0.9])
target = np.array([2.0, 1.0])
beta = np.linalg.norm((start**2 - target) / (2 * start), np.inf)
lipschitz = max(1 / start)
radius = 2 * beta / (1 + np.sqrt(1 - 2 * beta * lipschitz))
print(f"beta={beta:.6f}, L={lipschitz:.6f}, certified radius={radius:.6f}")
fig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(7, 4), layout="constrained")
for label, scale in [("Original equations", [1., 1.]), ("Rescaled equations", [1e4, 0.01])]:
    x = start.copy()
    distances = [0.0]
    for _ in range(5):
        residual = np.asarray(scale) * (x*x - target)
        jacobian = np.diag(2 * np.asarray(scale) * x)
        x -= solve_dense(jacobian, residual)
        distances.append(np.linalg.norm(x-start, np.inf))
    assert max(distances) <= radius and np.allclose(x**2, target)
    ax.plot(distances, "o-" if scale[0] == 1 else "x--", label=label)
ax.axhline(radius, color="black", ls=":", label="Guaranteed radius")
ax.set(xlabel="Newton iteration", ylabel="Distance from initial point (maximum norm)")
ax.legend()
fig.savefig("numerics-certified-newton.png", dpi=170)

Output from the reference run

beta=0.105556, L=1.111111, certified radius=0.112599

Tutorial on certification and damping

Compute the first majorant step and use it as a bound

Work through the solution

Scalar Newton at zero gives \(t_1=b\). The vector correction has size \(b\). After that move the distance to the root is at most \(r-b\), which can be evaluated without substituting a known root.

Change the starting point to a nearly singular configuration

Work through the solution

For \((1.5,0.1)\), the second correction is \(0.99/0.2=4.95\) and \(L=10\). The majorant gives no conclusion. For that coordinate a full step reaches \(5.05\), whereas halving the step decreases its size. The failure of the sufficient inequality leaves open whether an actual iteration converges.

Deuflhard · local and global Newton methods

Nonlinear systems and iterative methods · 7. Which correction does an experiment identify?

Separate parameter combinations visible in a nonlinear fit, pass a regular fold by continuation and decide when to refine a boundary-value mesh before trusting its Newton residual.

Solve only for directions constrained by data

Given covariance \(C>0\), whiten the residual \(r=C^{-1/2}(f(x)-d)\) and its Jacobian \(J\). Gauss–Newton minimises \(\|r+Js\|^2/2\). Write \(J=U\Sigma V^T\). Components orthogonal to its range cannot be changed to first order. Each reliably measured direction obeys \(v_i^Ts=-u_i^Tr/\sigma_i\). A direction in the nullspace is free. Setting its correction to zero selects the minimum Euclidean norm in the chosen parameter units.

\[s_\tau=-\sum_{\sigma_i>\tau}(u_i^Tr/\sigma_i)v_i,\qquad \|s_\tau\|\le\|r\|/\min_{\sigma_i>\tau}\sigma_i .\]

If the forward derivative carries error \(e_J\), singular values below \(\|e_J\|\) cannot be distinguished reliably from zero. Also compare predicted changes to measurement noise over physically allowed parameter displacements. Damping with a penalty gives factors \(\sigma_i/(\sigma_i^2+\lambda^2)\) and is continuous in the singular values. A penalty adds assumptions about scales.

Take two equal-noise readings whose model rows depend only on \(a+b\), with sensitivities \((1,1),(2,2)\). At \(a=b=0\) readings \((3,6)\) demand \(a+b=3\). Minimum norm assigns \(a=b=1.5\). Changing parameter units can change that individual assignment while retaining the identifiable sum. A new observation of \(a-b\) adds an independent singular direction.

Exchange a failing parameter for a curve coordinate

Let \(F(u,p)=0\) be \(n\) equations in \(n+1\) unknowns and suppose \([F_u,F_p]\) has row rank \(n\). Its nullspace supplies a unit tangent \(t\) oriented with the preceding tangent. Predict \(z_p=z_k+h t\). The corrector solves \(F(z)=0,\ t^T(z-z_p)=0\). At \(z_k\) the augmented derivative is invertible since its nullspace would lie both along \(t\) and perpendicular to it. This remains true locally for sufficiently small prediction steps, including a simple fold with singular \(F_u\).

For a boundary-value application consider \(u''+\lambda e^u=0,\ u(0)=u(1)=0\). A centred grid gives \(F_i=(u_{i-1}-2u_i+u_{i+1})/\Delta x^2+\lambda e^{u_i}\). The augmented rows have tridiagonal state derivative with diagonal \(-2/\Delta x^2+\lambda e^{u_i}\) and parameter column \(e^{u_i}\). Use grid-weighted state inner products for tangents so refinement does not change the meaning of arclength. Halve \(h\) and repeat the prediction if corrector displacements are too large relative to \(h\). If halving reduces the displacement by about four, the quadratic predictor defect is a geometrical effect.

How can a perfectly converged branch still predict a wrong maximum load?

Newton controls the residual of the chosen grid only. Correct the same branch point on a grid of half the spacing, matching arclength or a monotonic observed amplitude rather than load at the fold. In the regular second-order regime divide the fine–coarse output difference by three to estimate the fine-grid error. Refine the grid when this exceeds the output tolerance. Tighten the augmented linear and nonlinear solves enough that their estimated effect on that output is smaller.

Tutorial on a fold with an exact reference

Find a mesh-independent check for the boundary-value branch

Work through the solution

Set \(u(x)=2\log[\cosh(a/2)/\cosh(a(x-1/2))]\). Then \(u''=-2a^2\operatorname{sech}^2(a(x-1/2))\) and \(e^u=\cosh^2(a/2)\operatorname{sech}^2(a(x-1/2))\). Hence \(\lambda=2a^2/\cosh^2(a/2)\) and the end values vanish. This parametric curve can test either side of the turning point.

Locate the fold and choose a comparison observable

Work through the solution

Differentiating \(\log\lambda\) gives \(2/a-\tanh(a/2)\). Solve \(a\tanh(a/2)=2\), yielding \(a\) near 2.40 and \(\lambda\) near 3.51. The midpoint temperature \(2\log\cosh(a/2)\) is strictly increasing for positive \(a\), making it a well-defined matching observable across meshes.

Deuflhard · Gauss–Newton and continuation

Open this calculation with adjustable inputs

Download the executed OneSimulation notebook · Python source

The notebook reproduces the reference case and exposes the mesh or geometry parameters. Its saved output below comes from the actual OneSimulation solver.

"""Follow both Bratu branches using OneSimulation augmented Newton solves."""
import argparse
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
import numpy as np
from onesim.numerics.linear.matrices.dense_linear_algebra import solve_dense


def branch(parameter=2.4, intervals=16):
    if parameter <= 0 or intervals < 4 or intervals % 2:
        raise ValueError("Use a positive branch parameter and an even interval count >= 4")
    x = np.linspace(0, 1, intervals+1)
    exact = 2*np.log(np.cosh(parameter/2)/np.cosh(parameter*(x-.5)))
    reference = 2*parameter**2/np.cosh(parameter/2)**2
    state, load = exact[1:-1].copy(), reference
    h = 1/intervals
    for iteration in range(15):
        full = np.r_[0., state, 0.]
        residual = np.r_[(full[:-2]-2*state+full[2:])/h**2 + load*np.exp(state),
                         state[intervals//2-1]-exact[intervals//2]]
        if np.linalg.norm(residual) < 1e-10:
            break
        jacobian = np.zeros((intervals, intervals))
        jacobian[:-1, :-1] = (np.diag(-2/h**2 + load*np.exp(state))
                                  + np.diag(np.ones(intervals-2)/h**2, 1)
                                  + np.diag(np.ones(intervals-2)/h**2, -1))
        jacobian[:-1, -1] = np.exp(state)
        jacobian[-1, intervals//2-1] = 1
        correction = solve_dense(jacobian, -residual)
        state += correction[:-1]
        load += correction[-1]
    assert np.linalg.norm(residual) < 1e-8
    return x, np.r_[0., state, 0.], load, reference, np.linalg.norm(residual)


def experiment(parameter=2.4, intervals=16):
    x, state, load, reference, residual = branch(parameter, intervals)
    print(f"Analytical load: {reference:.9f}; discrete load: {load:.9f}")
    print(f"Augmented Newton residual: {residual:.3e}")
    refined = branch(parameter, 2*intervals)
    assert abs(refined[2]-reference) < abs(load-reference)
    print(f"Load error after doubling the mesh: {abs(refined[2]-reference):.3e}")
    fig = plt.figure(figsize=(10, 4.6), layout="constrained")
    view = fig.add_subplot(121, projection="3d")
    xx, aa = np.meshgrid(np.linspace(0, 1, 45), np.linspace(.3, 5, 45))
    lam = 2*aa**2/np.cosh(aa/2)**2
    field = 2*np.log(np.cosh(aa/2)/np.cosh(aa*(xx-.5)))
    view.plot_surface(xx, lam, field, color="#9dc6b9", alpha=.7, linewidth=0)
    view.plot(x, np.full_like(x, load), state, color="#ad491d", lw=2.5)
    view.set(xlabel="x", ylabel="Load lambda", zlabel="Temperature u", title="Folded family of 1D solutions")
    ax = fig.add_subplot(122)
    ax.plot(x, 2*np.log(np.cosh(parameter/2)/np.cosh(parameter*(x-.5))), label="Analytical same-midpoint profile")
    ax.plot(x, state, "o--", ms=3, label="Augmented OneSimulation solve")
    ax.set(xlabel="x", ylabel="Temperature u")
    ax.legend(fontsize=8)
    fig.savefig("complete-numerics-bratu.png", dpi=170)
    return load, residual


if __name__ == "__main__":
    parser = argparse.ArgumentParser(description=__doc__)
    parser.add_argument("--parameter", type=float, default=2.4)
    parser.add_argument("--intervals", type=int, default=16)
    args, _ = parser.parse_known_args()
    experiment(args.parameter, args.intervals)
    plt.show()

Analytical load: 3.513830356; discrete load: 3.506676842
Augmented Newton residual: 5.459e-13
Load error after doubling the mesh: 1.786e-03

Equilibria, branches and stability

Turn a physical stationarity condition into a nonlinear equation. Trace the solutions through loss of stability and decide which roots survive a perturbation.

  1. Bifurcation of a rotating hoop
  2. Why two coexisting phases share a tangent
  3. Mean-field ordering, response and the critical point
  4. When does a polar crystal change its preferred state?
  5. When a suspended capacitor loses its stable equilibrium
  6. Finite-size criticality and the evidence for a phase transition
  7. Phase nucleation, metastability and waiting-time statistics

Bifurcation of a rotating hoop

Classical mechanics

Use an effective potential to locate competing equilibria and predict the frequency of small oscillations on either side of a stability change.

From first principles

Find the instability threshold of the rotating hoop

Assume a frictionless bead constrained to a vertical circular hoop of radius \(R\). The hoop rotates at prescribed constant angular speed \(\Omega\) about its vertical diameter. With angle \(\theta\) measured upward from the bottom, the bead moves along the hoop at speed \(R\dot\theta\) and around the axis at speed \(\Omega R\sin\theta\). These directions are perpendicular, and its gravitational height above the bottom is \(R(1-\cos\theta)\). Thus its Lagrangian is

\[L=\tfrac12mR^2(\dot\theta^2+\Omega^2\sin^2\theta)-mgR(1-\cos\theta).\]

Apply the one-coordinate Lagrange equation. The rotation term tends to move the bead outward while gravity acts toward the bottom:

\[\ddot\theta=\sin\theta(\Omega^2\cos\theta-g/R).\]

Linearizing about the bottom gives

\[\ddot\theta\simeq(\Omega^2-g/R)\theta.\]

For \(\Omega^2<g/R\) the coefficient restores a small displacement; above threshold the bottom has growing small disturbances. Setting the exact right side to zero gives the additional equilibrium and its range:

\[\Omega_c=\sqrt{g/R},\]
\[\cos\theta_*=g/(R\Omega^2)\quad(\Omega\ge\Omega_c).\]

The two nonzero angles are related by reflection. Their existence follows from the prescribed rotation, so a freely slowing hoop would require an additional dynamical coordinate.

Derivation and applications

How high is the barrier between the two tilted equilibria?

In the rotating hoop, a new pair of stable angles appears where the bottom loses stability. Its curvature predicts a small-oscillation frequency; its depth determines whether a larger disturbance can carry the bead across the bottom. Consider a bead of mass \(m\) on a circular hoop of radius \(R\) spinning at prescribed constant angular speed \(\Omega\) about its vertical diameter. Ignore friction. Measure \(\theta\) upward along the hoop from the bottom. The vertical height change is \(R(1-\cos\theta)\) and the distance to the rotation axis is \(R\sin\theta\).

The constrained bead has kinetic energy \(mR^2(\dot\theta^2+\Omega^2\sin^2\theta)/2\). Collect the terms independent of \(\dot\theta\) as the potential governing motion in the rotating coordinate:

\[U_{\rm eff}(\theta)=mgR(1-\cos\theta)-\tfrac12mR^2\Omega^2\sin^2\theta .\]

Write \(s=\Omega^2R/g\) and differentiate. A tilted equilibrium exists only for \(s>1\). At either stable angle \(\theta_*\) substitute \(\cos\theta_*=1/s\) into the potential and factor the result:

\[U'_{\rm eff}=mgR\sin\theta(1-s\cos\theta),\]
\[U_{\rm eff}(\theta_*)=-mgR\,\frac{(s-1)^2}{2s}.\]

The bottom has \(U_{\rm eff}(0)=0\). Subtracting the energies gives the barrier and the threshold angular speed for a bead launched from a tilted minimum toward the bottom:

\[\Delta U=mgR\,\frac{(s-1)^2}{2s},\]
\[|\dot\theta|_{\rm th}=\sqrt{2\Delta U/(mR^2)}.\]

The velocity here is angular speed relative to the hoop. At threshold the trajectory approaches the unstable bottom asymptotically; crossing takes slightly more initial energy. Near \(s=1\) the barrier collapses quadratically even though two distinct equilibria already exist. The externally maintained rotation can exchange ordinary laboratory energy with the bead, while the effective one-coordinate energy is conserved.

Worked example

Estimate the speed needed to change branches

Change the inputs and investigate this derivation.

Take \(m=0.0500\) kg, \(R=0.200\) m and choose \(\Omega\) so that \(s=2.00\). The two minima then occur at \(\pm60.0^\circ\) and the barrier is

\[mgR/4=0.0245\,\mathrm{J}.\]

The tangential threshold speed is \(\sqrt{2\Delta U/m}=0.990\) m/s, or \(4.95\) rad/s along the hoop. A launch at \(0.800\) m/s has only \(0.0160\) J and returns to its original branch.

Second application

Use two measured angles to compare barrier depths

Change the inputs and investigate this derivation.

On the same hoop reduce the rotation until the two minima are at \(\pm36.9^\circ\). Their cosine is about \(0.800\), giving \(s=1.25\). The new barrier is \(mgR(0.25)^2/2.50=0.00245\) J, a tenth of the previous value. The new threshold tangential speed is \(0.313\) m/s. A smaller change in angle than this tenfold energy reduction might suggest is a measurable feature of a shallow double well.

Locate the bifurcation by measuring curvature

A vertical hoop rotates about its vertical diameter at a prescribed angular speed \(\Omega\). A frictionless bead can move along the hoop. Measure \(\theta\) from the bottom. The laboratory kinetic energy is \(mR^2(\dot\theta^2+\Omega^2\sin^2\theta)/2\). In the equation for the bead the effective potential per unit \(mgR\) is \(-\cos\theta-(\Omega^2R/2g)\sin^2\theta\). Its derivative factors as \(\sin\theta[1-(\Omega^2R/g)\cos\theta]\).

Open this experiment online

Plot the potential before and after crossing \(\Omega_c=\sqrt{g/R}\). At a stable equilibrium the slope vanishes and the curvature is positive. Above the critical speed the bottom loses positive curvature and two stable angles appear. The motor holding \(\Omega\) fixed exchanges angular momentum with the system. A freely rotating hoop with fixed total angular momentum describes a different experiment.

Reduce the constraint to one coordinate

A bead slides on a vertical circular hoop of radius \(R\). An external motor rotates the hoop at prescribed constant angular velocity \(\Omega\) about its vertical diameter. Let \(\theta\) be the angle from the lowest point. The bead’s height relative to that point is \(R(1-\cos\theta)\) and its distance from the rotation axis is \(R\sin\theta\). Assume negligible friction and a rigid constraint. The bead moves both along the hoop and azimuthally with it, even if \(\theta\) is momentarily stationary.

In laboratory coordinates its speed squared is \(R^2\dot\theta^2+\Omega^2R^2\sin^2\theta\). Subtract gravitational potential energy from this kinetic energy to construct a one-coordinate Lagrangian. Lagrange’s equation gives

\[\ddot\theta=\sin\theta(\Omega^2\cos\theta-g/R).\]

Although motion in the laboratory is constrained by a driven apparatus, the reduced equation is autonomous for constant \(\Omega\).

Track equilibria as the rotation rate changes

The torque vanishes at the bottom and the top because \(\sin\theta=0\). Off-axis equilibria also appear when \(\cos\theta=g/(\Omega^2R)\). They are possible only if the rotation exceeds \(\Omega_c=\sqrt{g/R}\). Each positive-angle equilibrium then has a negative-angle partner. The two have equal energies because the apparatus is symmetric.

For small displacement from the bottom,

\[\ddot\theta\simeq-(g/R-\Omega^2)\theta.\]

Below threshold the bottom is stable with angular frequency \(\sqrt{g/R-\Omega^2}\). As the threshold is approached this frequency tends to zero; the potential flattens. Above threshold the sign reverses and a small perturbation grows. Linearising about either displaced equilibrium instead gives a positive frequency squared \(\Omega^2\sin^2\theta_{\rm eq}\). Measuring this small-amplitude response distinguishes two predictions: where the bead rests and how steeply it is restored when disturbed.

Interpret the effective energy

The reduced motion admits the effective potential \(U_{\rm eff}=mgR(1-\cos\theta)-m\Omega^2R^2\sin^2\theta/2\). Its derivative predicts the reduced equation. Its negative centrifugal term differs in sign from the positive laboratory azimuthal kinetic energy. The motor supplies or absorbs work as the bead’s distance from the rotation axis changes. Treating the bead’s laboratory mechanical energy alone as conserved would therefore lead to the wrong equilibrium.

At exactly the critical rate, the quadratic terms in a small-angle expansion cancel and the first restoring term in \(U_{\rm eff}\) is positive and quartic. A finite disturbance can still oscillate there, but its period depends on its amplitude. An arbitrarily small oscillation cannot be assigned a finite harmonic frequency at that point.

Estimate the transition in a real apparatus

With \(R=0.250\) m and \(g=9.81\) m/s² the critical rate is 6.26 rad/s, or about 59.8 rpm. At 5.0 rad/s the bead returns to the bottom with small-oscillation frequency \(\sqrt{39.24-25}=3.77\) rad/s. At 8.0 rad/s the two new equilibria satisfy \(\cos\theta=39.24/64=0.613\), or \(\theta=\pm52.2°\). Small oscillations about either have angular frequency \(8\sqrt{1-0.613^2}=6.32\) rad/s.

When observing the transition, tilt of the axis or a small asymmetry of the hoop can favour one side. Hysteresis-like delays during a fast sweep can also arise from finite settling time in weak damping. Recording both equilibrium angles on slow sweeps and the independently measured oscillation frequency supplies a more discriminating test of the symmetric bifurcation than simply noting which side the bead occupies.

Break the symmetry with a weak bias

The two off-centre positions of the ideal rotating bead have equal energy. A small constant generalized torque \(\tau_b\) favouring positive \(\theta\) breaks that symmetry. Over a small working angular range it adds a term \(-\tau_b\theta\) to the effective potential. Write the original potential relative to its value at the bottom as \(mgR(1-\cos\theta)-m\Omega^2R^2\sin^2\theta/2\). Expanding near the critical rate \(\Omega_c^2=g/R\) gives quadratic coefficient \(mR^2(\Omega_c^2-\Omega^2)/2\). The quartic coefficient evaluated at threshold is \(mR^2\Omega_c^2/8\).

On the high-speed side define \(a=\Omega^2-\Omega_c^2>0\), \(b=\Omega_c^2/2\), and \(h=\tau_b/(mR^2)\). To leading order stationarity and local stability become

\[b\theta^3-a\theta-h=0,\qquad 3b\theta^2-a>0 .\]

With zero bias there are two symmetric minima and an unstable central position. Increasing positive bias deepens the positive minimum and eventually destroys the negative one. At the point where the negative minimum meets the adjacent maximum, both the stationarity equation and its derivative in \(\theta\) vanish. Thus

\[\theta_*=-\sqrt{a/(3b)},\]
\[h_*=(2a/3)\sqrt{a/(3b)}.\]

The critical bias scales as \(a^{3/2}\). Near the threshold a surprisingly small imperfection can remove one of the two resting positions entirely.

Estimate the bias needed to select a branch

Use a bead of mass \(0.0500\) kg on a hoop of radius \(0.250\) m. Then \(\Omega_c^2=39.24\) s⁻². Set \(\Omega^2=\Omega_c^2+0.600\) s⁻², making \(b=19.62\) s⁻². The unbiased displaced equilibrium angles are approximately \(\pm\sqrt{0.600/19.62}=\pm0.175\) rad. As positive bias is increased, the negative branch disappears near \(\theta_*=-0.101\) rad. The corresponding generalized acceleration is \(h_*=0.0404\) s⁻², giving \(\tau_b=mR^2h_*=1.26\times10^{-4}\) N m. A tangential force with moment arm \(R\) would supply about \(0.505\) mN.

This estimate uses both small angles and operation close enough to the critical speed that quartic truncation is accurate. An exact calculation differentiates the full trigonometric potential twice and solves the two equations simultaneously. In a slow sweep with weak damping, a bead initially on the unfavoured branch can remain there until its minimum disappears. Vibration or thermal agitation may make it switch earlier by crossing the remaining barrier. Repeating the sweep in both directions and at several bias amplitudes helps distinguish an imperfect constraint from random switching in a nearly symmetric system.

The same model predicts increasing sensitivity before an actual switch. Differentiate the cubic stationarity condition with respect to bias while following a particular equilibrium branch:

\[d\theta/dh=1/(3b\theta^2-a).\]

Its denominator is the local dimensionless restoring curvature. Thus a branch nearing disappearance both moves a large angular distance under a small bias increment and shows a very low small-oscillation frequency. Measuring those two effects on the same branch tests the local shape of the potential without requiring a precise measurement of the noisy switching moment. Away from a minimum the static slope can instead be negative, describing an unstable mathematical solution.

The sharp loss of a branch is best resolved with bias steps small compared with the measured interval between its creation and disappearance.

Where will a bead settle on a rotating hoop?

Choose zero potential at the bottom. Gravity contributes \(mgR(1-\cos\theta)\); the rotating-frame centrifugal term contributes \(-m\Omega^2R^2\sin^2\theta/2\). Inspect both the slope and the curvature at the bottom while increasing speed.
